Bayer HealthCare Pharmaceuticals Inc.
Tablets
20 mg orally twice daily with food
For the treatment of adult patients with advanced non-small cell lung
cancer (NSCLC) whose tumors have activating HER2 (ERBB2)
mutations, as detected by an FDA-approved test, and who have
received a prior systemic therapy.
Accelerated approval
For the treatment of adult patients with locally advanced or metastatic,
non-squamous non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) whose tumors
have activating HER2 (ERBB2) tyrosine kinase domain activating
mutations, as detected by an FDA-approved test, and who have
received a prior systemic therapy.

Cohort
Group

Dose level refers to a certain dose of sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) tested
during the Dose Escalation/Backfill parts of the study and can be further
investigated during the Dose Expansion part in the different groups.
A cohort is defined as participants assigned to the same dose level in the
Dose Escalation/Backfill parts of the study.
In Dose Expansion and Dose Extension parts of the study, the term
‘group’ refers to groups of participants with pre-specified criteria for cancer
characteristics and prior therapies.

Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0

1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Sevabertinib (HYRNUO) is a small molecule kinase inhibitor of human epidermal growth factor
receptor 2 (HER2, also known as Erb-B2 receptor tyrosine kinase 2 [ERBB2]), including wildtype HER2 and HER2 activating mutations. Sevabertinib is not currently approved for
marketing in any country.
The indication proposed by the Applicant is for the treatment of adult patients with advanced
non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) whose tumors have activating HER 2 (ERBB2) mutations,
as detected by an FDA-approved test, and who have received a prior systemic therapy.
The recommended dosage regimen for sevabertinib is 20 mg taken orally twice daily (BID) with
food until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence.
The submitted data provide substantial evidence of effectiveness to support the accelerated
approval of sevabertinib for the treatment of adult patients with locally advanced or metastatic
non-squamous NSCLC whose tumors have HER2 (ERBB2) tyrosine kinase domain (TKD)
activating mutations, as detected by an FDA-approved test, and who have received prior systemic
therapy.
The recommendation for accelerated approval is primarily supported by results from Study
21607 (SOHO-01), a non-randomized, open-label, single arm, multi-center, multi-cohort trial.
The primary efficacy populations considered by FDA comprise 122 patients with locally
advanced or metastatic, non-squamous NSCLC with HER2 (ERBB2) TKD mutations who had
received at least one line of prior systemic therapy and received sevabertinib 20 mg orally BID in
SOHO-01. This primary efficacy population was comprised of two groups: patients naïve to antiHER2 therapy (Group D) and patients that had progressed on prior anti-HER2 directed therapy
(Group E). Across the two groups, most patients received prior platinum-based chemotherapy
(94% and 77% in Groups D and E, respectively), immunotherapy (71% and 56% in Groups D
and E, respectively), or platinum-based chemotherapy in combination with immunotherapy (69%
and 56% in Groups D and E, respectively). In Group D (n=70), the objective response rate
(ORR) per blinded independent central review (BICR) per Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid
Tumors (RECIST) version 1.1 was 71% (95% confidence interval [CI]: 59, 82), and the median
duration of response (DOR) was 9.2 months (95% CI: 6.3, 15.0), with 54% of responders having
an observed DOR ≥ 6 months and 18% of responders having an observed DOR ≥ 12 months. In
19
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 219972
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
Group E (n=52), the ORR per BICR per RECIST version 1.1 was 38% (95% CI: 25, 53), and the
median DOR was 7.0 months (95% CI: 5.6, not estimable [NE]), with 60% of responders having
an observed DOR ≥ 6 months.
The demonstrated response rates in the primary efficacy populations are considered clinically
meaningful in the context of available therapy for the indicated patient population.
Additional supportive information derives from strong mechanistic evidence of HER2 mutations
as molecular drivers of NSCLC.
The submitted evidence meets the statutory evidentiary standard for accelerated approval in
accordance with the provisions of 21 CFR 315.510 Subpart H. Given that in the absence of
therapy, HER2-mutated NSCLC tumors do not typically regress, ORR can be directly attributed
to an effect of the drug; in this disease setting, ORR is considered an intermediate endpoint
reasonably likely to predict clinical benefit. There are no therapies with traditional approval
specifically for patients with HER2 mutated NSCLC whose disease has progressed after standard
first-line therapy, which includes platinum-based chemotherapy and/or an anti-PD-(L)1 antibody.
Fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan-nxki (T-DXd), a HER2-directed ADC which demonstrated an ORR
of 58% in a population similar to the Group D efficacy analysis population in this application, is
currently under accelerated approval. Standard treatment options for the indicated population are
otherwise the same as those used for a biomarker unselected population of patients with NSCLC
and include single agent chemotherapy (e.g., ORR in the range of 10-15% for docetaxel) or
docetaxel plus ramucirumab (ORR of 23% [95% CI: 20, 26]). The effect of sevabertinib based on
magnitude and duration of responses observed in the primary efficacy populations in SOHO-01
demonstrates a meaningful advantage over available therapies for this previously treated patient
population.
Continued approval for this indication may be contingent upon verification and description of
clinical benefit in a confirmatory trial(s). The trial intended to verify the clinical benefit of
sevabertinib is Study 22615 (SOHO-02), an open-label, randomized, controlled, global study of
sevabertinib versus standard of care pembrolizumab plus platinum-based doublet chemotherapy
in patients with previously untreated, unresectable locally advanced or metastatic non-squamous
NSCLC harboring HER2 TKD mutations. The primary efficacy endpoint is progression-free
survival (PFS) per RECIST 1.1 by BICR; key secondary endpoints include ORR and overall
survival (OS). The Consolidated Appropriations Act (2023) provides FDA with the authority to
require that confirmatory studies to verify clinical benefit be underway prior to accelerated
approval. SOHO-02 is 60% enrolled as of October 2025, and topline data from the primary PFS
analysis are expected Q1 2027. As such, the review division considers the confirmatory trial for
this accelerated approval to be underway.

Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment

Lung cancer is the leading cause of cancer-related death in the United States (U.S.), with approximately 226,650 new cases of lung cancer
expected to be diagnosed in 2025, and approximately 124,730 deaths attributed to lung cancer (Cancer Facts & Figures, 2025). Non-small cell
lung cancer (NSCLC) accounts for 85% of lung cancers, and 44% to 48% of patients with NSCLC present with stage IV/metastatic disease at the
time of diagnosis (Duma 2019; Ganti 2021). HER2 (ERBB2) mutations occur in approximately 2% of all NSCLC and are primarily activating
mutations involving Exon 20 of the HER2 (ERBB2) gene. Patients with HER2-mutated NSCLC are more likely to be female, non-smokers, and
have tumors with adenocarcinoma histology (IASLC, 2023). These patients often develop central nervous system (CNS) disease, with brain
metastases reported in up to 47% of patients (Offin, 2019). Median survival in patients with metastatic HER2-mutated NSCLC is 17 to 23 months
(Pillai, 2017). There are no products targeting HER2-mutated NSCLC currently under traditional FDA approval. Initial systemic therapy in the
metastatic setting generally consists of platinum-based doublet chemotherapy, with or without anti-PD-(L)1 antibody. Available treatment
options for patients who experience disease progression following first-line therapy are the same as those used for a biomarker unselected
population of patients with NSCLC and include single agent chemotherapy (e.g., docetaxel with ORR 10-15%), combination chemotherapy (e.g.,
docetaxel plus ramucirumab with ORR 23%), or anti-PD-(L)1 antibody as a single agent for patients who are anti-PD-(L)1 naïve (ORR 15-20%).
Fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan-nxki (T-Dxd), a HER2-directed antibody and topoisomerase inhibitor conjugate, was granted accelerated approval
for NSCLC with activating HER2 mutations in August 2022 (ORR 58%). Metastatic HER2-mutated NSCLC remains a fatal, incurable disease,
and an unmet need exists for additional systemic therapies for patients with metastatic NSCLC harboring HER2 mutations whose disease has
progressed after first-line therapy.
Sevabertinib is a small molecule kinase inhibitor of HER2, including wild-type HER2 and HER2 activating mutations. The recommended
dosage regimen for sevabertinib is 20 mg taken orally twice daily with food until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. Sevabertinib is not
currently approved for marketing in any country.
The primary study supporting this application is the SOHO-01 (Study 21607), a non-randomized, open-label, single arm, multi-center, multicohort trial of sevabertinib in patients with locally advanced, unresectable or metastatic solid tumors with HER2 aberrations, with the dose
expansion part of the trial (“Phase Ib”) evaluating sevabertinib in patients with locally advanced, unresectable or metastatic HER2-mutated

NSCLC. The primary efficacy outcome measure for this marketing application is confirmed ORR according to RECIST v1.1 as assessed by
BICR, with DOR by BICR.
The primary efficacy populations considered by FDA comprise 122 patients with locally advanced or metastatic, non-squamous NSCLC with
HER2 TKD mutations who had received at least one line of prior systemic therapy and received sevabertinib 20 mg orally BID in two groups of
SOHO-01. Among 70 patients who had not received prior HER2-directed therapy (Group D), 94% had received prior platinum-based
chemotherapy and 69% had received previous treatment with an anti-PD-1/anti-PD-L1 antibody. The ORR per BICR was 71% (95% CI: 59, 82)
and the median duration of response (DOR) was 9.2 months (95% CI: 6.3, 15.0ong), with 54% of responders having an observed DOR ≥ 6
months and 18% of responders with an observed DOR ≥ 12 months. Responses were generally consistent across subgroups.
Among 52 patients with locally advanced or metastatic, non-squamous NSCLC with HER2 TKD mutations who had received prior systemic
treatment with platinum-based chemotherapy and a HER2-targeted ADC (Group E) the ORR was 38% (95% CI: 25, 53), with a median DOR of
7.0 months (95% CI: 5.6, NE) and 60% of responders having a DOR ≥ 6 months.
The Applicant submitted data from patients with squamous histology and those with tumors with HER2 non-TKD mutations; however, the
sample sizes in these populations were very limited, and response rates were numerically lower (see Section 8.1.1 for further details). FDA
considered that there was insufficient evidence of efficacy in this population, and the indication for sevabertinib considered in FDA’s review was
limited to the treatment of adult patients with locally advanced or metastatic non-squamous NSCLC whose tumors have HER2 TKD activating
mutations and who have received prior systemic therapy.
Sevabertinib has an acceptable safety profile when assessed in the context of a life-threatening disease. The assessment of safety at the
recommended dosage was based on data from patients with locally advanced or metastatic non-squamous NSCLC who received sevabertinib 20
mg BID in SOHO-01 (n=268; refer to Section 8.2). The safety population used to inform Section 6 of product labeling includes patients in with
locally advanced or metastatic non-squamous NSCLC with HER2 mutations enrolled in Groups D and E (n=136). In the pooled safety
population, the most common (≥20%) adverse reactions were diarrhea, rash, stomatitis, and paronychia. The most common (≥2%) Grade 3 or 4
laboratory abnormalities were decreased potassium, increased lipase, decreased lymphocyte, decreased sodium, increased amylase, increased
ALT, and increased AST. In the safety population for Groups D and E, the most common adverse reactions (>20%) in patients who received
HYRNUO were diarrhea, rash, paronychia, stomatitis, and nausea. The most common Grade 3 or 4 laboratory abnormalities (≥ 2%) were
potassium decreased, lipase increased, lymphocyte count decreased, sodium decreased, amylase increased, aspartate aminotransferase (AST)

sevabertinib, based on data from the pooled safety population, are diarrhea, hepatotoxicity, interstitial disease (ILD)/pneumonitis, ocular toxicity,
and pancreatic enzyme elevation. These safety concerns are adequately addressed in the Warnings and Precautions section and dose modification
recommendations included in the product label. There were no significant safety concerns identified during NDA review requiring risk
management beyond labeling or warranting consideration for a Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS). Sevabertinib will be prescribed
by oncologists who are familiar with monitoring, identifying, and managing the toxicities described in the USPI.
A supplementary PMA (sPMA) application for the Life Technologies Corporation (subsidiary of Thermo Fisher Scientific) Oncomine™ Dx

that the data submitted in the sPMA are supportive of approval of the ODxTT as a companion diagnostic device to identify patients with locally
advanced or metastatic non-squamous NSCLC harboring HER2 (ERBB2) TKD activating mutations who may be eligible for treatment with
sevabertinib, and the sPMA will be approved contemporaneously with this NDA.
The submitted evidence meets the statutory evidentiary standard for accelerated approval of sevabertinib for the treatment of adult patients with
locally advanced or metastatic non-squamous NSCLC whose tumors have HER2 TKD activating mutations and who have received prior
systemic therapy. The benefit-risk profile in the indicated population is considered favorable based on the magnitude and durability of the
observed responses with sevabertinib, which are consistent with a clinically meaningful advantage over available therapies for an indicated
patient population that has a life-threatening disease and an unmet medical need. Sevabertinib has an acceptable safety profile when assessed in
the context of a life-threatening disease and considering the observed efficacy results in patients with non-squamous NSCLC whose tumors have
HER2 TKD activating mutations.

Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
Lung cancer is the most common cancer and the leading cause of cancer deaths, with an
estimated 2.5 million new cases (12.4% of all cancer diagnoses) and 1.8 million deaths (18.7%)
worldwide in 2022 (1). In the US, an estimated 234,580 new cases of lung cancer were
diagnosed in 2024, with 125,070 deaths from lung cancer (2).
NSCLC
NSCLC is the most common type of lung cancer (3), accounting for ≥85% of lung cancer cases
(4-6). NSCLC is a heterogeneous disease where malignant cells are formed primarily in the lung
parenchyma. It is a general term for several different types of cancer in the lungs, including
adenocarcinoma, squamous cell carcinoma, large cell (undifferentiated) carcinoma, and other
rare forms. NSCLC primarily originates in the epithelial cells of the lungs where
adenocarcinoma develops in the peripheral lung tissue while squamous cell carcinoma develops
near the central bronchus. The histologic type of NSCLC correlates with the site of origin,
reflecting the variation in respiratory tract epithelium of the bronchi to alveoli (7). The overall 5year survival rate of NSCLC patients is approximately 16% (8, 9), while it is much lower in
patients with advanced NSCLC, ranging from 2.8% to 14.6% (8, 9). Nearly 75% of patients with
NSCLC are diagnosed at an advanced stage.
HER2 activating mutations in NSCLC
Receptor tyrosine kinases (RTKs) regulate important biological processes, including cell
proliferation, differentiation, metabolism, and survival, by activating an array of downstream
signaling pathways. The ERBB family of RTKs consists of 4 members: EGFR (also known as
ERBB1/HER1), ERBB2 (HER2), ERBB3 (HER3), and ERBB4 (HER4) (10). Mutations within
the kinase domain of HER2 and EGFR are common oncogenic driver events in NSCLC (11).
Activating driver mutations in HER2 have emerged as novel targets for treating NSCLC. These
mutations are found in 2% to 4% of advanced NSCLC in Caucasian and Asian populations, with
the vast majority being HER2 ex20ins (96%) and a minority consisting of point mutations (11,
12). HER2 activating mutations have been associated with female sex, Asian ethnicity, nonsmoking status, adenocarcinoma histology, and younger patients, with a median age of
approximately 60 years (13). In other tumor types (e.g. breast, ovarian, and bladder cancer)
HER2 ex20ins mutations are less frequent (majority < 1%) (14). Other activating point mutations
in HER2 can be detected as well, and in some tumor types those are more frequent than HER2
ex20ins (15). Overall, patients with HER2-mutant NSCLC have a worse prognosis (median OS
29
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
of 1.6 to 1.9 years from time of diagnosis) than NSCLC with EGFR or ALK mutations partially
due to the lack of effective targeted therapies (16).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s analysis of the condition. HER2 (ERBB2) mutations occur in
approximately 2% of all NSCLC and are primarily activating mutations involving Exon 20 of the
HER2 (ERBB2) gene. Patients with HER2-mutated NSCLC are more likely to be female, nonsmokers, and have tumors with adenocarcinoma histology (IASLC, 2023). These patients often
develop central nervous system (CNS) disease, with brain metastases reported in up to 47% of
patients (Offin, 2019). Median survival in patients with metastatic HER2-mutated NSCLC is 17
to 23 months (Pillai, 2017).

Analysis of Current Treatment Options
The Applicant’s Position:
Currently, targeted therapies are the standard treatment for most oncogene-driven NSCLC
patients. The international guidelines for metastatic NSCLC indicate that subtyping of NSCLC,
as well as testing for mutations, is necessary for effective treatment decision-making (17, 18).
However, no targeted therapy has received full approval specifically for the treatment of patients
with NSCLC, whose tumors harbor HER2 mutations. Therefore, the standard treatment options
for these patients are the same as those used for patients with NSCLC without a specific
mutation.
In the absence of approved targeted therapies, platinum-based chemotherapy, with or without
immunotherapy, is the recommended first-line treatment option for patients with HER2-mutant
metastatic NSCLC. However, the outcome of patients treated with upfront chemotherapy (ORR
of 36% and median PFS of 5.1 months; (18) or immuno-chemotherapy (ORR of 37% and
median PFS of 7.1 months; 19) is still unsatisfactory and may be further improved. For patients
with progression of disease following platinum-based chemotherapy, standard treatment options
include either single agent docetaxel or docetaxel in combination with ramucirumab, which have
been associated with ORRs of 6% to 23%, or single agent anti-PD-L1 therapy if not received in
the first-line setting, with ORRs of 14% to 20% (see Table 1).
HER2-targeted therapies have demonstrated inconsistent results in the second-line setting (1922). The results from irreversible pan-HER TKIs (afatinib, dacomitinib, neratinib, and
poziotinib) have been disappointing (23). The ORR was 28% and the median PFS was
5.5 months for poziotinib.
To date, ADC trastuzumab deruxtecan (Enhertu) is the only HER2-targeted treatment for
NSCLC patients (Table 1). On 11 AUG 2022, the FDA granted accelerated approval to
trastuzumab deruxtecan for the treatment of adult patients with unresectable or metastatic
30
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5697816

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 219972
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
NSCLC whose tumors have activating HER2 mutations, as detected by an FDA-approved test,
and who have received a prior systemic therapy.
The efficacy results from the interim analysis (N=52) of the Phase 2 DESTINY-Lung02 study
provided the first clinical data of 5.4 mg/kg trastuzumab deruxtecan in patients with previously
treated HER2-mutated NSCLC and served as the basis for accelerated approval by the FDA. The
confirmed ORR was 57.7% and the median DOR was 8.7 months (Table 1). At the primary
analysis (N=102), the ORR was 49.0% and the median DOR was 16.8 months (24). Safety was a
concern, since adjudicated drug-related ILD occurred in 12.9% of patients and resulted in the
death of one patient. Drug-related TEAEs of Grade ≥3 were reported in 38.6% of patients. Dose
interruption, reduction, and treatment discontinuation were reported in 44.6%, 17.8%, and 14.9%
of patients, respectively (24).
Therefore, despite these recent advances, there is an urgent need for more effective and better
tolerated agents that can improve response rates and response durability, eventually delaying
progression and improving survival, in NSCLC patients with HER2 activating mutations.

a: Immune-mediated adverse reactions, e.g. pneumonitis, colitis, hepatitis and hepatotoxicity, endocrinopathies, nephritis with renal dysfunction, dermatologic
adverse reactions.

Source: Latest available USPI for Enhertu, Opdivo, Keytruda, Tecentriq, Cyramza, and Taxotere.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the available therapies for patients with metastatic NSCLC with HER2 (ERBB2)
mutations. There are no products targeting HER2-mutated NSCLC currently under traditional approval in the U.S. Initial systemic
therapy in the metastatic setting generally consists of platinum-based doublet chemotherapy, with or without anti-PD-(L)1 antibody.
Available treatment options for patients who experience disease progression following first-line therapy include single agent
chemotherapy (e.g., docetaxel), combination chemotherapy (e.g., docetaxel plus ramucirumab), or anti-PD-(L)1 antibody as a single
agent for patients who are anti-PD-(L)1 naïve. As noted by the Applicant, Fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan-nxki (T-Dxd), a HER2directed ADC, was granted accelerated approval for the treatment of patients with NSCLC whose tumors have activating HER2
mutations and who have received prior systemic therapy in August 2022. Zongertinib, a tyrosine kinase inhibitor, was granted
accelerated approval on August 8, 2025, for the treatment of adult patients with unresectable or metastatic non-squamous non-small

3 Regulatory Background
US Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
The Applicant’s Position:
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) has had an active IND Application (155685) in the US since AUG
2021 and is not currently registered (or approved) in any region. An overview of the key
regulatory milestones during the clinical development of sevabertinib is provided in Section 3.2.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. Sevabertinib is not currently approved in the United
States or other regions for any indication.

were positive for HER2 (ERBB2) TKD activating mutations, 7.3% (6/82) were unevaluable, and
there were no samples with negative status for HER2 (ERBB2) TKD activating mutations.

Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
Sevabertinib is a reversible oral tyrosine kinase inhibitor that targets human epidermal growth
factor receptor 2 (HER2), including HER2 exon 20 insertion mutations and HER2 point
mutations. It also exhibits activity against wild-type (WT) epidermal growth factor receptor
(EGFR) and certain EGFR mutants. The indication is for the treatment of adult patients with
locally advanced or metastatic non-squamous non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) whose
tumors have HER2 (ERBB2) tyrosine kinase domain activating mutations, as detected by an
FDA-approved test, and who have received a prior systemic therapy. The recommended dose
and schedule of sevabertinib for patients is 20 mg orally twice daily (BID).
The established pharmacological class for sevabertinib is kinase inhibitor. Predictive X-ray
crystallography showed that sevabertinib noncovalently binds to the HER2 kinase domain. In in
vitro kinase activity inhibition assays, sevabertinib inhibited the activity of WT HER2 and WT
EGFR with IC50 values of 2.94 nM and 0.15 nM, respectively. In cellular assays, sevabertinib
inhibited the in vitro proliferation of cells overexpressing WT HER2 (IC50 = 5.30 nM) and a
panel of HER2 exon 20 insertion mutations and activating point mutations (IC50 = 0.54 to 13.9
nM) at concentrations below the free Cmax of 88 nM at the recommended dose of 20 mg BID
based on ~95% protein binding. Sevabertinib also inhibited phosphorylated HER2 (p-HER2) and
downstream signaling in cancer cells harboring an HER2 exon 20 insertion or WT HER2
amplification. In vivo, sevabertinib exhibited antitumor activity against NSCLC patient-derived
xenograft (PDX) models harboring the HER2 exon 20 insertion YVMA.
Secondary pharmacology studies showed that sevabertinib exhibited off-target activity in
functional in vitro assays at the human adenosine A1 receptor and motilin receptor at
concentrations ≥46-fold the free Cmax of 88 nM at the recommended clinical dose. In kinase
activity competition assays, sevabertinib inhibited discoidin domain receptor 2 (DDR2) with an
IC50 of 53.3 nM, DDR1 with an IC50 of 111 nM, and mitogen-activated protein kinase 14
(MAPK14) with an IC50 of 132 nM, indicating that sevabertinib has the potential to target these
kinases at clinically relevant concentrations. To assess sevabertinib’s effects on cardiac function
due to the potential off-target activity on human adenosine and motilin receptors, ex vivo rabbit
Langendorff heart preparations showed concentration-dependent decrease in left ventricular
pressure (LVP), contractility, and coronary flow. Sevabertinib also shortened the QTcF interval
and the left ventricular monophasic action potential duration at 90% repolarization (MAPD90)
and caused widening of the QRS interval. Effects were noted at doses up to 113-fold higher than
the free Cmax of 88 nM.
In a GLP-compliant study, sevabertinib inhibited hERG potassium current in vitro with an IC50
of 2.36 μM (26-fold the free Cmax of 88 nM). In other in vitro assays, sevabertinib and its major
41

Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
metabolite, M1, did not interfere with cardiac ion channels (hNav1.5, hCav1.2, hKir2.1,
hKvLQT1), with the exception of the hERG potassium current (IC50 = 2.2 μM and 3.5 μM,
respectively). In an in vivo cardiovascular safety pharmacology study, oral administration of up
to 15 mg/kg sevabertinib did not increase the mean QT/QTc interval; however, individual
animals displayed slightly prolonged (up to ~8 ms) QT/QTc intervals at about 1.5 to 4 posttreatment at ~12-fold the free Cmax. Cardiac arrhythmia (including QT prolongation) was
observed in patients treated with the recommended clinical dose of 20 mg BID and has been
reported with other dual EGFR and HER2 inhibitors; refer to analysis and discussion presented
in Sections 6, 8.2.3, and 8.2.4.5.. In a central nervous system (CNS) safety pharmacology study
in rats, single oral administration of 15 and 45 mg/kg sevabertinib led to increased rearing
activity at 1 and 24 hours postdose. There were no effects on respiratory parameters in
respiratory safety studies conducted in rats, and sevabertinib did not affect CNS or respiratory
parameters in the 4-week repeat-dose toxicity study in rats.
No unique human metabolites were identified. The major human metabolite of sevabertinib, M1
(approximately 31% to 38% of the parent drug), showed weaker cellular activity than
sevabertinib and is unlikely to contribute to clinical activity due to the differences in
pharmacokinetics, the unbound fraction, and potency. The M1 metabolite exhibited off-target
effects on the adenosine A1 and A3 receptors, adenosine transporter, cyclooxygenase-2, and 15lipoxygenase at concentrations ≥2.7-fold the free Cmax in humans at the recommended clinical
dose. The M1 metabolite was present in rat and monkey plasma. Sevabertinib was rapidly and
widely distributed throughout rat tissues with the highest concentrations found in the liver,
adrenal cortex, kidney (outer medulla and cortex), and blood vessel wall (aorta), gastrointestinal
(GI) tract, and thyroid gland, but brain penetration was low. There was notable distribution of
sevabertinib to the melanin-containing tissues of the eye and skin with long-term retention in the
eye tissue (see ocular toxicity discussed below). In consultation with the FDA Clinical
Pharmacology team, animal to human exposure multiples were calculated using the mean steady
state Cmax and AUC0-12h of 902 ng/mL and 6640 ng*h/mL, respectively, at the recommended
dose in humans. For comparisons with animal AUC0-24, AUC0-12h in humans were multiplied by
2.
The Applicant evaluated the safety of sevabertinib administered orally once daily in 4- and 13week good laboratory practice (GLP)-compliant general toxicology studies using rats and
cynomolgus monkeys. The oral route of administration is consistent with the intended clinical
route of administration. In the 4- and 13-week studies, treatment with sevabertinib caused
mortalities. All high-dose (15 mg/kg) females in the 4-week rat study, all high-dose (8 mg/kg)
males in the 4-week monkey study, and two high-dose (5 mg/kg) animals in the 13-week
monkey study were sacrificed early due to rapid weight loss and diarrhea, as well as poor general
condition. Histological findings of GI toxicity included erosion/ulcer, atrophy/degeneration,
inflammation, immune infiltrate, and hemorrhage. Additional target organs in both species
included the skin (wounds and scab formation; histological findings of up to moderate
erosion/ulcer, atrophy, crust, hyperplasia, and inflammation), liver (up to marked fat
accumulation), and pancreas (acinar cell atrophy). GI, skin, liver, and pancreatic toxicities are
42

consistent with clinical experience. Additional target organs in the monkey included the thymus
(up to severe atrophy, severe decreased adipose tissue), and mesenteric lymph node (marked
decreased adipose tissue, slight increased cellularity).
Ocular toxicity was observed in toxicology studies. In rats, treatment with ≥10 mg/kg
sevabertinib (≥0.2 times the human exposure at the recommended dose of 20 mg BID based on
AUC) resulted in slight atrophy of the lacrimal gland and minimal optic nerve hemorrhage. In
monkeys, minimal lens degeneration occurred at 5 mg/kg (0.1 times the human exposure at the
recommended dose based on AUC). In a non-GLP dose range-finding study in rats, treatment
with ≥5 mg/kg sevabertinib (0.1 times the human exposure at the recommended clinical dose
based on AUC) induced minimal to moderate atrophy/degeneration of the corneal epithelium. In
the clinic, most ocular toxicity was characterized as dry eye, and there was one case of unilateral
blindness. Ocular toxicity is commonly seen with EGFR inhibitors and has also been seen with
dual EGFR and HER2 inhibitors.
Carcinogenicity studies were not conducted for sevabertinib and are not warranted for the
proposed indication. Sevabertinib was not mutagenic in an in vitro bacterial reverse mutation
assay and was not clastogenic or aneugenic in in vitro and in vivo micronucleus tests. Based on
the totality of the data, M1 is not genotoxic. Sevabertinib also did not demonstrate phototoxic
potential in a GLP-compliant in vitro assay with mouse fibroblasts.
The Applicant conducted GLP-compliant embryo-fetal development (EFD) studies in pregnant
rats to assess the effect of sevabertinib on embryonic and fetal development. Sevabertinib was
orally administered once daily to pregnant females during organogenesis (Gestation Days [GD]
6–17) at doses of 3, 7, and 11 mg/kg in the preliminary EFD study and at doses of 1.5, 3, and 6
mg/kg in the pivotal EFD study. Overall, sevabertinib caused dose-dependent maternal and fetal
toxicity. Sevabertinib doses ≥6 mg/kg (≥0.18 times the human exposure at the recommended
dose based on AUC) resulted in maternal toxicity characterized by decreases in body weight,
body weight gain, and food consumption. Higher doses of sevabertinib (≥7 mg/kg) caused more
maternal toxicity with additional adverse clinical signs (diarrhea, piloerection, thinness, and
hunched posture) and early euthanasia due to morbidity in three animals at 11 mg/kg.
Sevabertinib doses ≥6 mg/kg (≥0.18 times the human exposure at the recommended dose based
on AUC) resulted in significantly reduced fetal body weights and decreased placenta and gravid
uterine weights. The Applicant was unable to achieve higher exposures due to dose-limiting
toxicities in pregnant rats, under the conditions tested, and only limited variations were observed.
The Applicant also provided a weight-of-evidence (WOE) assessment, including literature and a
new approach methodology (NAM), for reproductive risk to supplement the rat EFD study,
consistent with the FDA Guidance for Industry, “Oncology Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive
Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations.” Animal data from the literature demonstrate
that HER2 and EGFR are critically important for reproductive and developmental processes
including blastocyst implantation, placental development, cardiac and neural development, and
embryofetal/postnatal survival and development (see Section 5.4.4 for detailed assessment). The

Anti-proliferative activity of sevabertinib in a HER2-mutant Ba/F3 cell line panel
The cellular activity profile of sevabertinib was evaluated using proliferation assays on a panel of
Ba/F3 murine pro-B cells that stably overexpress a broader panel of mutant variants of the
human HER2 genes.
Sevabertinib showed strong potency in the most frequent HER2 exon20 insertion mutations
(IC50s for the most prevalent exon20 insertion mutations: Ba/F3-HER2 Y772_A755dup: 8.31
nM; Ba/F3-HER2 G776delinsVC: 6.73 nM; and Ba/F3-HER2 G778_P780dup: 2.34 nM).
Similarly strong antiproliferative activity was observed in the Ba/F3 cells carrying the most
frequent HER2 activating point mutations: Ba/F3-HER2 S310F: IC50=2.47 nM; Ba/F3-HER2
S310Y: IC50=0.98 nM; Ba/F3-HER2 L755P: IC50=5.29 nM (for additional mutations, see
Table 3).
Sevabertinib was equipotent on Ba/F3-HER2wt vs. the mutant variants, with an IC50 of 5.3 nM
(Table 3). Additional HER2 mutations have been profiled and are documented in
Module 4.2.1.1, Report B003066.

The cellular activity profile of sevabertinib in different EGFR- and HER2 mutational
backgrounds was assessed using proliferation assays on a panel of Ba/F3 murine pro-B cells that
stably overexpress mutant variants of the human EGFR or HER2 genes.
Sevabertinib showed strong potency in various EGFR exon20 insertion mutations and sub-nM
potency in the classical activating mutations (EGFR ex19del and L858R) (IC50s for the most
prevalent exon20 insertion mutation EGFR ex20insASV: 4.66 nM; EGFR ex19del: 0.16 nM; and
EGFR L858R: 0.52 nM). Sevabertinib was also highly active in Ba/F3-HER2 ex20ins cells with
an IC50 of 8.31 nM. Sevabertinib retained its cellular potency in the presence of the acquired
resistance mutation C797S (EGFR ex19del/C797S: IC50=0.22 nM), but significantly lost activity
in the presence of the gatekeeper mutation T790M (EGFR ex19del/T790M: IC50=21.9 nM)
(Table 4).
Activity of sevabertinib was significantly reduced in Ba/F3-EGFRwt cells (IC50=221 nM),
resulting in selectivity of the target mutations over EGFRwt of ~47-fold for the exon20ins
mutations (EGFR ex20insASV vs. EGFRwt) and of ~1380-fold for the classical activating
mutations (EGFR ex19del vs. EGFRwt). In contrast to the observed selectivity on Ba/F3EGFRwt, sevabertinib was equipotent on Ba/F3-HER2wt vs. the mutant variant (Table 4).
Additional EGFR mutations have been profiled and are documented in Module 4.2.1.1, Report
PH-42058.

In vivo antitumor efficacy of sevabertinib in the patient-derived NSCLC xenograft model
CTG-2543 (HER2 Y772 A755dup [ex20insYVMA]) in mice
HER2 ex20ins mutations represent the vast majority (96%) of HER2 activating mutations in lung
cancer and are found in 2-4% of advanced NSCLC (11). The in vivo efficacy of sevabertinib was
evaluated in the patient-derived, HER2 Y772_A755dup (ex20insYVMA) positive NSCLC
model CTG-2543, to assess the therapeutic potential in HER2 ex20ins mutant patients (25).
Female athymic Nude-Foxn1nu mice were subcutaneously transplanted with CTG-2543 tumor
fragments. Starting at a tumor size of 150-300 mm3, the animals were treated with either vehicle
(control) or sevabertinib at 10 mg/kg, 25 mg/kg, or 50 mg/kg QD for 28 days. Animal body
weight and tumor size were measured twice weekly and at study end, the tumors were collected
for tumor weight analyses. Treatment with sevabertinib was well tolerated and resulted in
significant tumor growth inhibition compared to the vehicle control and tumor regression with
final T/Cvol/weight values of 0.27/0.22 (10 mg/kg QD), 0.15/0.11 (25 mg/kg QD), and 0.12/0.05
(50 mg/kg QD), respectively (Figure 1) (Module 4.2.1.1, Report R-13868).

Anti-proliferative activity of BAY 3306572 (M1 of sevabertinib) in a Ba/F3 EGFRmut and
HER2mut cell line panel
BAY 3306572 has been identified as the major human metabolite (M1) of sevabertinib.
The compound was profiled for its in vitro anti-proliferative activity in a panel of Ba/F3 EGFR
mutant and HER2 mutant cell lines.
BAY 3306572 showed a median activity of 58.6 nM (ranging from 13.3 nM to 100 nM) in all
profiled HER2ex20ins mutations and of 29.9 nM (ranging from 2.99 nM to 236 nM) in all
profiled HER2 point mutations.
Compared to the parent compound, sevabertinib, the activity of BAY 3306572 was ~ 13-fold
weaker in HER2ex20ins mutations and ~6-fold weaker in HER2 point mutations (sevabertinib:
median activity in HER2ex20ins: 4.53 nM and HER2 point mutations: 5.09 nM)
(Module 4.2.1.1, Report B003066) (see also Table 5).
BAY 3306572 also shows weaker activity, as compared to the parent compound sevabertinib, in
Ba/F3 cells expressing EGFRwt and HER2wt. The IC50s are 558 nM and 53 nM for BAY
3306572 (Table 5) vs. 221 nM and 5.3 nM (Table 4) for sevabertinib, respectively.
Additional EGFR and HER2 mutations have been profiled and are documented in
Module 4.2.1.1, Report B003067.

Reference ID: 5697816

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 219972
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
In order to test for interferences with cardiac ion channels in vitro, the effects of sevabertinib and
its metabolite, BAY 3306572, on the hNav1.5 Na+ current (involved in cardiac depolarization),
the L-type hCav1.2 Ca2+, hKir2.1 K+, hKvLQT1 K+, and hERG K+ currents (involved in cardiac
repolarization) were investigated using automated voltage-clamp systems. Sevabertinib and BAY
3306572 did not interfere with these cardiac ion channels, with the exception of the hERG K+
current which was inhibited by sevabertinib with an IC50 of 2.2 µmol/L; this concentration is
about 24-fold higher than the human plasma concentration (Cmax,u,ss ~91 nmol/L) at the clinically
relevant dose of 20 mg/patient BID. BAY 3306572 inhibited the hERG K+ current with an IC50
of 3.5 μmol/L (Module 4.2.1.2, Report PH-42086 and Module 4.2.1.2, Report B002455).
The specificity of sevabertinib was evaluated in the KinaseProfiler panel at
. Out of 373
kinases tested, sevabertinib (at 1µM) inhibited 18 kinases by more than 80%, including 3 EGFR
kinase family members (EGFR1, ErbB2, and ErbB4). These kinases were included in follow-up
studies. The most potently inhibited non-EGFR kinase was DDR2, with an IC50 of 53.3 nM (2
mM ATP) (Module 4.2.1.1, Report R-13813 and Module 4.2.1.1, Report PH-42059).
(b) (4)

Ex vivo, sevabertinib, at up to 1 µmol/L, did not alter hemodynamic and electrical parameters in
rabbit Langendorff hearts. At 10 µmol/L; however, left ventricular systolic pressure and
contractility were markedly reduced, and ECG changes (QTcF shortening and QRS widening)
were observed (Module 4.2.1.2, Report PH-42110).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s conclusions. However, FDA calculated the Cmax
of unbound sevabertinib to be ~88 nM at the recommended dose of 20 mg orally twice daily
based on a total Cmax of 902 ng/mL and fraction unbound of 4.71%. Additional pertinent
details are provided below.
•

In functional in vitro assays assessing off-target activity, sevabertinib exhibited
antagonistic effects at the human adenosine A1 receptor and human motilin receptor
with IC50 values of 4.3 and 4.9 µM, respectively (≥49-fold the free Cmax of 88 nM in
humans at the recommended clinical dose of 20 mg BID) (Report Nos. R-13833 and
R-13933).

•

In in vitro TR-FRET-based kinase activity inhibition or competition assays,
sevabertinib inhibited DDR2 with an IC50 of 53.3 nM, DDR1 with an IC50 of 111 nM,
and MAPK14 with an IC50 of 132 nM, indicating that sevabertinib has the potential to
target these kinases at clinically relevant concentrations based on a free Cmax of 88
nM. Sevabertinib inhibited the other seven non-EGFR kinases evaluated with IC50
values ranging from 335 nM to 5.2 μM (Report No. PH-42059).

•

In an ex vivo study, exposure of spontaneously beating rabbit Langendorff heart to
sevabertinib at concentrations of 0.1, 1, and 10 µM resulted in a concentrationdependent decrease in LVP, dP/dtmax (contractility), and coronary flow, which
reached statistical significance compared to the control at 10 μM (or ≥1 μM for LVP).
54
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Reference ID: 5697816

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 219972
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
Sevabertinib also shortened the QTcF interval and left ventricular MAPD90, and
caused widening of the QRS interval. Overall, the largest changes were observed at
10 μM (Study No. T104629-2; Report No. PH-42110), a concentration much higher
than the free Cmax of 88 nM at the recommended clinical dose. These findings also
provide relevant safety pharmacology information on vascular effects due to the offtarget effects of adenosine and motilin receptors.
Safety Pharmacology
Effects of sevabertinib on vital organ systems, i.e. cardiovascular, respiratory, and the CNS, were
investigated in one in vitro (hERG assay) and in three in vivo studies, according to the ICH S7A
and ICH S7B guidelines. The in vivo studies were conducted after single oral administration of
sevabertinib (i) in rats at doses of 0 (Ethanol/KolliphorHS15®/Water [10/40/50, v/v/v], pH 4), 5,
15, and 45 mg/kg; and (ii) in telemetered dogs at doses of 0 [ethanol/PEG400/water, 1:5:4, pH
~4, 2 mL/kg], 2, 5, and 15 mg/kg body weight. Concordant with the ICH S9 guideline, the
effects of sevabertinib on safety pharmacology endpoints of CNS function (detailed clinical
observations following dosing) were assessed within a pivotal 4 week, toxicity study in Wistar
rats, with once daily oral administration. The studies were conducted in compliance with the
OECD Principles of GLP at certified testing facilities in Germany or the United Kingdom, which
are member countries of the OECD Mutual Acceptance of Data Agreement.
Sevabertinib, at single oral doses, was devoid of major acute effects (i) on hemodynamic
parameters in a dog telemetry study (Module 4.2.1.3, R-14051), (ii) on parameters of CNS
function (behavior, locomotor activity, and body temperature) in rats (Module 4.2.1.3, PH42619), and (iii) on parameters of respiratory function (respiration rate, and tidal and minute
volume) in rats (Module 4.2.1.3, Report PH-42756).
Detailed clinical observations as included in the 4-week, repeat-dose, rat toxicity study did not
reveal abnormalities in general behavior, posture, and breathing on Days 1 and 2 (Module
4.2.3.2, Report PH-42111).
As shown in vitro (hERG K+ assay) and in vivo (ECG, including QT/QTC intervals in telemetry
dogs), sevabertinib has a potential for delayed repolarization at clinically relevant plasma
concentrations and above. A hERG IC50 value of 2.4 µmol/L was calculated (Module 4.2.1.3, R13892), resulting in a no-effect level (defined as 1/30 of the hERG IC50) of 80 nmol/L; this is
similar to the human plasma concentration (Cmax,u,ss ~91 nmol/L) at the clinically relevant dose of
20 mg/patient BID. At the high dose in dogs, the QT/QTc interval in the ECG was slightly (~8
ms), but significantly prolonged at about 1.5 h to 4 h post-treatment. This delayed repolarization
was observed at concentrations about 12-fold higher than the human plasma concentration
(Cmax,u,ss ~91 nmol/L) at the clinically relevant dose of 20 mg/patient BID (Module 4.2.1.3,
Report R-14051).

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 219972
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of the cardiovascular safety
pharmacology findings. However, FDA notes that the prolonged QT/QTc interval occurred in
individual animals (Study No. T104913-8, Report No. R-14051). Sevabertinib showed CNS
effects in an in vivo safety pharmacology study in rats (Study No. T105511-3; Report No. PH42619). After single administration of oral sevabertinib at doses of 5, 15, and 45 mg/kg, animals
in the mid- and high-dose groups showed increased rearing activity at 1 and 24 hours postdose
compared to the vehicle control group. In addition, animals at the high dose also showed
decreased body temperature at 1-hour postdose. In the 4-week repeat-dose toxicity studies in
rats, once daily administration of sevabertinib at doses up to 15 mg/kg did not cause CNS or
respiratory effects.

ADME/PK
The Applicant’s Position:
The PK of sevabertinib following IV administration was characterized by high systemic
clearance in mice and a moderate clearance in rats and dogs. The volume of distribution was
high in all three species. Elimination of sevabertinib was bi-phasic with short apparent half-lives
in mice, rats and dogs and long terminal half-lives in mice and rats. Following oral
administration to rats and dogs, the tmax was short. Oral bioavailability was low at 1 mg/kg in rats
and moderate at 1 mg/kg in dogs and 10 mg/kg in rats. [14C]sevabertinib (and/or its labelled
metabolites) was rapidly and widely distributed from the blood compartment to most peripheral
organs and tissues, as shown by QWBA in rats. Brain penetration was very low and testes
penetration of radioactivity was moderate. In addition, no radioactivity could be detected in fetal
tissues when administered to pregnant rats. In pigmented rats, there was evidence of a specific
affinity to melanin bearing tissues. Data from IV and po administration of [14C]sevabertinib to
rats suggest that the predominant clearance pathways of sevabertinib can be attributed to
metabolic processes. Excretion of [14C]sevabertinib-derived radioactivity after IV dosing to BDC
cannulated rats was found in bile, feces, GIT and urine. In addition, radioactivity was detected in
the milk of lactating rats.
Binding of sevabertinib and the major human plasma metabolite M1 (BAY 3306572) to plasma
proteins was moderate across species, with a stronger binding of M1 to human plasma
(sevabertinib: 95.3%; M1: 98.1%). In the whole blood distribution assay, the Cb/Cp ratio for
sevabertinib and M1 was more bound to plasma proteins (human), or rather equally distributed
(preclinical species). In human hepatocytes in vitro and from the mass balance study in humans,
the oxidation of sevabertinib leading to metabolite M1, is the most important metabolic pathway,
followed by several minor oxidations and oxidations with subsequent secondary reactions. No
human specific metabolites were identified. Metabolism of sevabertinib in human hepatocytes
was mainly catalyzed by CYP3A4 and to a much lesser extent by CYP1A1 and glucuronidation.

R-14142: Quantitative and Qualitative Whole-Body Autoradiography after Oral and
Intravenous Administration of [14C]sevabertinib to Male and Female Rats
[14C]sevabertinib (and/or its labelled metabolites) was rapidly and widely distributed from the blood
compartment to most peripheral organs and tissues, as shown by QWBA in rats. Post-orally, most
maximum concentrations in organs/tissues were higher than the blood maximum concentration.
This was also reflected in terms of AUCs. However, brain penetration was very low and testes
penetration of radioactivity was moderate. In pigmented rats, there was clear evidence of a specific
affinity of substance-associated radioactivity to the eye wall and the skin as melanin bearing
tissues.
Overall, there was no evidence of retention of radioactivity in the organs and tissues of male and
female Wistar rats and of male pigmented rats with the exception of melanin-bearing tissues, in
particular the eye wall.

Sevabertinib: Determination of Lacteal Excretion and Plasma Pharmacokinetics in Postpartum
Female Albino Wistar Rats Following a Single Oral Administration of [14C]sevabertinib (Report
B003512)
Mean milk to plasma concentration ratios determined for each time point ranged from 12.6 at 1 h to 26.1 at 6 h.
The cumulative sum percent of administered dose secreted into milk from 0 to 32 h was 1.3%, suggesting that
[14C]sevabertinib-derived radioactivity penetrates from circulating blood into milk of lactating female rats.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 219972
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s conclusions; however, these studies were not
reviewed in detail by the nonclinical review team. Notable findings include that following oral
administration of 2 mg/kg radiolabeled sevabertinib to rats, the greatest concentration was
observed in the eye (ciliary body, choroid, and iris) and skin of pigmented animals. In albino
rats, sevabertinib showed poor distribution in the eye. At 168 hours, postdose radioactivity in the
skin declined; however, in the eyes of pigmented rats, a high concentration of radioactivity was
observed at 672 hours postdose, suggesting not only affinity of sevabertinib for melanincontaining tissue but also retention of the drug in the eye. Additional organs that retained
radiolabeled drug up to 168 hours postdose included the thyroid, intestine, and liver. In addition,
results from lactating rats administered a single dose of [14C] sevabertinib demonstrating
sevabertinib-derived radioactivity was measured in milk was included in the label. Studies of
drug–drug interactions were reviewed under the Clinical Pharmacology section.

Toxicology
General Toxicology
The nonclinical safety testing strategy for sevabertinib was developed in accordance with the
ICH S2(R1), M3(R2), S3A, S5(R3), S7A, and S9 guidelines. All nonclinical studies were
conducted in accordance with the best scientific principles. Pivotal nonclinical studies were
conducted in conformance with GLP; 21 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR), Part 58 and/or the
principles of OECD GLP in countries that are part of the OECD Mutual Acceptance of Data
process. Rats and cynomolgus monkeys were used in the toxicology program; both species are
considered to be pharmacologically active and have a metabolism of sevabertinib comparable to
that of humans. Dogs could not be used because of insufficient exposure due to enzyme
induction after repeated dosing.
The toxicity, the reversibility of effects, and exposures of sevabertinib were assessed in repeat
dose, toxicology studies, up to 13 weeks duration in rats and monkeys. Daily oral administration
of sevabertinib to rats and monkeys over 2, 4, or 13 weeks revealed adverse effects characterized
by general signs of toxicity, such as reduced food intake, changes in laboratory parameters,
diarrhea, and degenerative and inflammatory processes in epithelial and mucosal tissues. The
highest doses tested in the 13-week studies correspond to MoEs slightly less than 1 for unbound
AUC and greater than 2 for unbound Cmax compared to exposures at the maximum therapeutic
dose of 2 x 20 mg per day.
Most relevant histopathological findings in the dose range finder studies, and the pivotal 4- and
13-week studies in rats were degenerative and inflammatory processes in epithelial and mucosal
tissues, such as forestomach (erosion/ulcer, atrophy, cyst, and inflammation), glandular stomach
(atrophy), duodenum (villous atrophy), jejunum (villous atrophy and Paneth cell hypertrophy),
ileum (villous atrophy and lymphangiectasis), cecum/colon (degeneration/atrophy, inflammatory
cell infiltrates, and dilation), and rectum (degeneration/atrophy). In addition, thymus atrophy and
67

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 219972
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
displayed fur thinning or some hairless areas on the forelegs or fur thinning at
the neck.
In the open field observation, the animals did not show any relevant clinical
symptoms up to the highest dose level of 10 mg/kg/day.
Body Weight

Body weight development within the main groups was mostly comparable to the
respective control groups up to the dose of 10 mg/kg/day sevabertinib in males
and up to 5 mg/kg/day in females during the dosing period. A slight reduction in
body weight gain was observed in female animals dosed with 10 mg/kg/day.
During the recovery period, an increased body weight was observed in both
sexes when compared to controls indicating reversibility.

Feed Consumption

The food intake profiles were roughly comparable between the main groups
and their respective control groups up to the high dose of 10 mg/kg/day
sevabertinib. Statistically significant increases in water consumption were
observed in males and females dosed with sevabertinib. But due to the low
quantity of deviations and lack of a dose dependency, the changes in water
intake are not related to the test item.

Ophthalmoscopy

No adverse findings

Hematology

A statistically significant increase in mean neutrophil counts and monocyte
counts starting at 2.5 mg/kg were observed in male and female animals. No
increase in neutrophiles and monocytes was noted at the end of the recovery
period in males and females, indicating full reversibility.
Several parameters (erythrocytes, hematocrit, mean corpuscular hemoglobin
concentration, hemoglobin concentration, thromboplastin time, and
lymphocytes) displayed statistically significant deviations from the controls.
However, due to an absence of dose dependency and/or all individual values
lying within the historical control range, these alterations are not considered
toxicologically relevant.

Coagulation

No adverse findings

Clinical chemistry

A slight statistically significant reduction in protein and albumin levels was
noted in males starting at a dosage of 2.5 mg/kg/day up to the dosage of
10 mg/kg/day. A statistically significant decrease in protein and albumin levels
was still noted at the end of the recovery period in males dosed with
10 mg/kg/day. Among females, only the animals administered a dosage of
5 mg/kg/day showed a statistically significant deviation in protein levels
compared to the respective control group. At the end of the recovery period, a
slight increase in GGT and LDH levels was observed. However, these changes
were minimal and lacked biological relevance. In addition, all other liver
enzymes remained within the normal ranges, indicating no toxicological
relevance.
Several other parameters (Glucose, P, and CA) displayed statistically
significant deviations from the controls. Due to an absence of dose dependency
and/or just minimal changes within the biological range, these alterations are
not considered toxicologically relevant. A statistically significant reduction in
creatine kinase levels was observed among females in the main group who
received a dosage of 10 mg/kg/d sevabertinib. Usually, a decrease in creatine
kinase activity is not typically associated with toxicity.

Urinalysis
No adverse findings
Gross Pathology

Necropsy did not reveal any test item related findings up to the dose of 2.5
mg/kg/day. A crust on the head was recorded for one male animal from the 5
mg/kg/day group, during necropsy in animals from the main group. In addition,
wounds in the cervical or flank region were recorded for 2 males dosed at 10
mg/kg/day from the recovery groups.

Organ weights

The animals from the main group showed a statistically significant increase in
relative thymus weight (% body weight); more specifically there was an
approximately +21% increase in females dosed at 10 mg/kg/day. The mean
relative thymus weights were also slightly increased in the groups of females
dosed at 2.5 mg/kg/day (approximately +12 %) and 5 mg/kg/day (approximately
+13 %).
When compared to brain weight, an increase of mean relative thymus weight
(approximately + 21%) was observed for females dosed at 10 mg/kg/day, but
this was not statistically significant.
Organ weight determination in animals from the recovery group showed a
statistically significantly decrease in mean absolute kidney weight in the group
of females dosed at 10 mg/kg/day. Due to the minor quantity of the deviation (8.9 % compared to control mean) and the slight reduction of body weights in
this dose group the deviation was not regarded as toxicologically relevant.

Histopathology

At terminal sacrifice, minimal epidermal atrophy was recorded in the skin of the
back region in males from the main group starting at 5 mg/kg/day (5 mg/kg/day:
2 out of 10 animals; 10 mg/kg/day: 8 out of 10 animals). Epidermal atrophy was
characterized by a thinner appearance of the epidermis and less kerato-hyaline

globules in the stratum granulosum. A full recovery was observed for epidermal
atrophy 4 weeks post treatment. However, two recovery males, dosed at 10
mg/kg/day, revealed findings of the skin. Minimal epidermal hyperplasia and
slight dermal inflammatory cell infiltration in the flank region were recorded in
one male animal. Another male animal showed moderate multifocal epidermal
hyperplasia and ulceration, crust formation and dermal inflammation in the
cervical region at recovery necropsy. In addition, one male main group animal,
dosed at 5 mg/kg/day, was diagnosed with crust formation at terminal sacrifice.
The corresponding histopathological findings were similar to those observed in
the previously mentioned animals. Due to the low incidences of ulcerative skin
lesions, the association with treatment with sevabertinib is questionable, as
they can also represent a background change. A relation to treatment with
sevabertinib, however, cannot be excluded. Minimal epidermal atrophy was
also recorded for sevabertinib-treated Wistar rats in the previous 4-week
systemic toxicity study. The effects on the skin are considered to be related to
the mode of action (inhibition of EGFR and HER2) of sevabertinib.

Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
Mortality

Premature euthanasia of two high dose animals on day 11 and day 85, respectively,
due to pronounced weight loss (-14.8% and -16.4%) and watery feces and transiently
reddish feces color/occasionally reddish or mucous admixtures.
Euthanasia of one high dose male due to scrotal herniation of the small intestine (not
considered test item related).

Clinical Signs

Slight to severe changed feces consistency/diarrhea small amounts of reddish or
mucous admixtures and a reddish color of feces

Body Weight

Slight weight loss (individual animals) at 2.5 mg/kg/day
Up to marked body weight loss (individual animals) at 5 mg/kg/day

Feed Consumption

Reduced food intake (up to severe)

Ophthalmoscopy

No findings

Hematology

In animals no. 8 (male) and 20 (female) dosed at 5 mg/kg/day and who were
prematurely sacrificed due to body weight loss accompanied by changed feces
consistency, the erythrocyte counts, hemoglobin concentration, hematocrit and
thrombocyte counts (only animal no. 20) were slightly increased at blood collection on
day 11 (for animal no. 20) or 85 (for animal no. 8) compared to previously determined
individual values of these parameters. For all other animals that underwent scheduled
necropsy, hematology and coagulation parameters were inconspicuous and no
statistically significant deviations were recorded for groups dosed at 2.5 and 5
mg/kg/day.

Coagulation

-

Clinical chemistry

The prematurely euthanized animal no. 08 (male, 5 mg/kg/day) had an increase of
alkaline phosphatase at blood collection on day 85. In animal no. 20 (female, 5
mg/kg/day), an increase in total bilirubin was recorded on day 11 prior to premature
sacrifice of this animal: On day 11, total bilirubin was approximately 2.5-times higher
compared to the individual total bilirubin value during the predose phase.
No further deviations of clinical chemistry parameters were regarded as related to
treatment with sevabertinib.

Urinalysis

No findings

Gross Pathology

Small intestine showed distension, discoloration, obstruction

Organ weights

At the 2.5 mg/kg/day dose level, a statistically significant decrease in mean relative
liver weight was recorded in males; however, without any histopathological correlate.

Histopathology

Histopathological evaluation revealed a marked to massive atrophy of mediastinal and
mesenteric adipose tissue and predominantly mononuclear inflammation of marked
severity in the stomach and slight severity in the large intestine in one animal dosed at
5 mg/kg/day, who was prematurely sacrificed on dosing day 11. In Peyer’s patches
and the mesenteric lymph node of this animal, a reactive increase in cellularity was
recorded. The small intestine showed a slight increase in mononuclear cell infiltrates,
only.
Similar gastrointestinal histopathological findings were observed in a male animal
dosed at 5 mg/kg/day, who was prematurely sacrificed on day 85: slight inflammation
was found in the stomach, cecum, colon and rectum, and cellularity was slightly
reactively increased in Peyer’s patches. Moreover, a massive atrophy was present in
the thymus. Testes showed minimal germ cell degeneration. Moderate sperm
decrease and slight cell debris was found in the epididymides. Prostate gland and
seminal vesicles were markedly atrophic. This effect was considered as secondary to
the reduced general condition of this prematurely sacrificed animal. Thymic atrophy
was observed in males and females at 5 mg/kg/day and in one female at 2.5

The toxicokinetic evaluation revealed no sex-related differences in exposure. For
sevabertinib, Cmax was reached 2 to 4 h after administration. For its metabolite, BAY
3306572, Cmax was reached 4 h after administration. At 24 hours after administration,
the concentrations of both analytes were below 3.5% of the respective Cmax with a
trend towards lower values for the metabolite. On days 28 and 91, the exposure in
animals from both dose groups in terms of AUClast and Cmax was moderately to
markedly higher than on day 1. Exposure in terms of both, AUClast and Cmax was at
least 1.5-fold higher than on day 1.

Toxicokinetics

The exposure of sevabertinib on day 91, in terms of AUC(0-24), was 1290 μg*h/L for
females and males and the corresponding Cmax was 247 μg/L for both sexes.
Under steady state conditions (day 28 and 91), the exposure of BAY 3306572, the M1
metabolite, represented between approximately 80% to 95 % of the exposure to the
unchanged drug sevabertinib in terms of AUClast and between 75% to approx. 79 % in
terms of Cmax.
For the summary of exposure in steady state (Day 28 and 91) to sevabertinib in male
& female monkeys, please refer to Table 18.
For the summary of exposure in steady state (Day 28 and 91) to BAY 3306572 (M1 of
sevabertinib) in male & female monkeys, please refer to Table 19.

Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
In the GLP-compliant 4-week repeat-dose toxicity study in monkeys (reviewed under IND
155685), sevabertinib was orally administered at 3, 6, or 10/8 mg/kg once daily. Due to severe
liquid and/or soft feces associated with body weight loss that did not respond to preventative
care, the high dose level was reduced from 10 to 8 mg/kg in males beginning on Day 11. Since
the health status of these animals did not improve, all monkeys at 8 mg/kg were euthanized
prematurely on Day 18. Target organs included the GI tract (parasite, up to mild infiltrate, mild
inflammation, minimal atrophy), skin (mild crust, moderate ulcer) thymus (up to mild atrophy),
eye (up to mild infiltrate), and bone marrow (sternum; minimal lymphoid aggregate).
FDA limited the review of the 2-week non-GLP-compliant dose-range finding study in rats to
ocular effects (Study No. T104669-6, Report No. PH-41998). Rats were orally administered
sevabertinib at doses of 5, 15, or 30 mg/kg once daily. Treatment with ≥5 mg/kg sevabertinib
induced minimal to moderate atrophy/degeneration of the corneal epithelium
5.4.2

Genetic Toxicology

The Applicant’s Position:
Sevabertinib was not mutagenic in a GLP-compliant bacterial reverse mutation assay (Ames
assay) in the presence and absence of Aroclor 1254-induced rat liver S9 fractions at
concentrations up to 5,000 μg/plate.
The aneugenic and clastogenic potential of sevabertinib was assessed in vitro in a GLPcompliant micronucleus test. Based on toxicity, the limit concentrations for assessment were set
at 75 µg/mL (without S9 mix, 4 hours treatment) and 85 µg/mL (with S9 mix, 4 hours
treatment). In addition, concentrations up to 30 µg/mL were chosen for assessment of the
extended treatment without the S9 mix. Evaluation of the data indicate that sevabertinib is not
genotoxic in the micronucleus test in vitro, when tested up to cytotoxic concentrations in the
absence or presence of metabolic activation.
In addition, there was no evidence of a biologically relevant or statistically significant genotoxic
effect of orally administered test item in the peripheral blood of the micronucleus test in the male
and female rats when tested in assessable doses up to the MTD in the course of the pivotal 4week toxicology study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA confirmed the Applicant’s results of the submitted genotoxicity assays and agrees with the
conclusions. Standard positive controls confirmed the sensitivity and validity of these GLPcompliant assays. The genotoxic potential for metabolite M1 was not investigated by the
Applicant; however, we note that M1 was present following incubation of sevabertinib with
hepatocytes from rat and monkey and measured in plasma of animals in the 13-week repeat-dose
studies. Additionally, M1 is structurally similar to sevabertinib, which was not genotoxic in in
vitro and in vivo assays. Therefore, M1 is not genotoxic based on the totality of data with active
pharmaceutical ingredient and metabolite (genotoxicity, ADME, and structure).

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 219972
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
Carcinogenicity
Carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted. Consistent with the guideline ICH S9,
carcinogenicity studies are not warranted to support clinical trials for therapeutics intended to
treat patients with advanced cancer.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees.
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
Dedicated fertility and early embryonic development studies were not conducted and are not
warranted per ICH S9 guidance for nonclinical safety assessment of pharmaceuticals intended
for the treatment of advanced cancer. Reproductive organs were weighed and/or examined
microscopically in repeat-dose rat and monkey toxicity studies.
A preliminary embryo-fetal development study in rats was conducted. Oral (gavage)
administration of 0, 3, 7, or 11 mg/kg/day sevabertinib to pregnant female Wistar rats during
organogenesis resulted in test item-related effects at all dose levels, including three mortalities
following administration of 11 mg/kg/day. A dose level of 7 or 11 mg/kg/day was considered
excessive due to the severity of the test item-related body weight loss, reduced food
consumption, adverse clinical observations, and reduced fetal weights, however no fetal
malformations or variations were noted. Test item-related effects following administration of 3
mg/kg/day were confined to initial reduced body weight gain and small reductions in food
consumption and considered not to represent an adverse effect to the pregnant animals. The
marginal reductions in fetal weight were not classified as adverse. A no observed adverse effect
level (NOAEL) of sevabertinib was established as 3 mg/kg/day as a result.
In the pivotal EFD study in rats, sevabertinib-related effects observed after administration of 1.5
or 3 mg/kg/day were limited to marginal reductions in body-weight gain and food consumption.
This resulted in slightly lower absolute body weights at GD 21 compared with controls and did
not adversely affect maternal health or fetal development. Fetal effects were limited to slightly
reduced fetal weights (combined and individual sexes), resulting in a slight reduction in gravid
uterine and placental weights; however, mean values were within the historical control data
range. A dose level of 6 mg/kg/day was considered excessive due to the severity of the
sevabertinib-related body weight changes, reduced food consumption, and reduced fetal and
placental weights. In summary it can be stated that no fetal malformations or adverse fetal
variations were noted at unbound exposures of sevabertinib at multiples of exposure below or in
the range of 1 compared to the therapeutic human unbound exposure. Based on these findings,
the NOAEL is 3 mg/kg/day.
Rabbit did not qualify as a test species for embryo-fetal development assessment, as no relevant
exposure to sevabertinib could be achieved.

Toxicokinetics

Toxicokinetics
