Discussion on Reproductive Toxicology
A preliminary EFD study in rats was conducted. Oral (gavage) administration of 0, 3, 7, or 11
mg/kg/day sevabertinib to pregnant female Wistar rats during organogenesis resulted in test
item-related effects at all dose levels, including three mortalities following administration of 11
mg/kg/day. A dose level of 7 or 11 mg/kg/day was considered excessive due to the severity of
the test item-related body weight loss, reduced food consumption, adverse clinical observations,
and reduced fetal weights, however no fetal malformations or variations were noted. Test itemrelated effects following administration of 3 mg/kg/day were confined to initial reduced body
weight gain and small reductions in food consumption and considered not to represent an adverse
effect to the pregnant animals; and non-adverse marginal reductions in fetal weights.
In the pivotal EFD study in rats, sevabertinib-related effects observed after administration of 1.5
or 3 mg/kg/day were limited to marginal reductions in body-weight gain and food consumption.
This resulted in slightly lower absolute body weights at GD 21 compared with controls and did
not adversely affect maternal health or fetal development. Fetal effects were limited to slightly
reduced fetal weights (combined and individual sexes), resulting in a slight reduction in gravid
uterine and placental weights, but mean values were within the historical control data range. A
99
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5697816

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 219972
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
dose level of 6 mg/kg/day was considered excessive due to the severity of the sevabertinibrelated body weight changes, reduced food consumption, and reduced fetal and placental
weights; however, no fetal malformations or adverse fetal variations were noted.
An evaluation in rabbits was not possible. Several studies in rabbits with oral application of
sevabertinib in different formulations did not lead to any relevant systemic exposure. The reason
for the poor exposure is probably due to a low absorption in the intestine in combination with a
very fast metabolism and rapid clearance of the test substance. In vitro experiments with rabbit
hepatocytes suggest rapid metabolism. In order to achieve an exposure that corresponds to the
therapeutic exposure in humans, slow-bolus intravenous application in rabbits was tried as an
alternative. This study ultimately had to be discontinued because intravenous application led to
unacceptable local effects, which finally resulted in the premature euthanasia of the animals.
Although there is no evidence of developmental toxicity from the rat EFD studies, there is a clear
concern that EGFR and HER2 inhibition may have adverse effects on embryo-fetal development.
These concerns include target expression (EGFR and HER2) in reproductive organs and the
pharmacological pathway, along with its critical role in cell growth, survival, and differentiation
within development. Additionally, effects observed in EGFR and HER2 knock-out animals and
an in vitro study performed with human inducible pluripotent stem cells suggest potential
teratogenic effects. After discussion between the FDA and Bayer, a weight-of-evidence based
assessment of the potential for sevabertinib to cause reproductive and embryofetal toxicity is
provided.
The EGFR and the HER2 (ERBB2) receptor are widely expressed throughout the body in
various tissues and organs, such as the skin, nerves, lungs, breast, prostate, bladder and
gastrointestinal tract ((27) and in house data). Notably, they are also present in reproductive
organs, including the ovaries and uterus, where they play important roles in cell growth and
differentiation.
The EGFR plays a crucial role in embryogenesis and organogenesis, especially with its
expression observed in the placenta, uterus, and nervous system. Studies involving knockout
models reveal that mice lacking EGFR suffer from significant developmental issues, leading to
mid-gestation death due to placental dysfunction and abnormal organ formation (28-31).
Specifically, the absence of EGFR results in fewer spongiotrophoblast layers in the placenta,
which negatively impacts fetal growth and survival. Newborns lacking EGFR display
underdeveloped features, such as immature lungs and skin defects, which align with EGFR
expression patterns. Underdeveloped lungs with collapsed alveoli, preventing newborns from
beginning or maintaining respiration, resulted in mortality due to respiratory distress syndrome
and respiratory failure. Mice also showed significant growth retardation, along with eye defects
(born with open eyes) and hair abnormalities (30, 32). Progressive neurodegeneration began
shortly after birth in areas such as the frontal cortex, olfactory bulb and thalamus. This effect was
characterized by increased cellular apoptosis and a near absence of viable neurons (33).
100
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5697816

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 219972
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
Zhang et al. (2013) (34) underscores the critical role of EGFR in the regulation of cartilage
matrix degradation and secondary ossification center formation, which is essential for normal
skeletal development. EGFR regulates these processes through both β-catenin-dependent and independent pathways. The findings reveal that while chondrocyte hypertrophy occurs normally,
there is impaired mineralization and apoptosis of hypertrophic chondrocytes in the absence of
EGFR signaling. Research by Large et al. (2014) (35) explored the role of EGFR in endometrial
function during early pregnancy. Using conditional knockout models in mice and siRNA in
human endometrial stromal cells, the study found that EGFR is essential for successful
decidualization and overall fertility. Mice with conditional EGFR ablation experienced
significant subfertility, with pregnancy failures occurring shortly after the implantation of the
blastocyst. The findings also extended to primary human endometrial stromal cells, highlighting
EGFR as a critical regulator of human decidualization.
Similarly, the HER2 is vital for the development of cardiac and neural tissues, particularly in
forming cardiac trabeculae and cranial sensory ganglia. Knockout studies show that mice
deficient in HER2 face severe cardiac and neural developmental defects, leading to embryonic
lethality (28, 36, 37). In addition, HER2/HER4 heterodimers have been implicated in the
development of mammary glands (38). The absence of HER2 results in significant neural defects
and a loss of Schwann cells, which are essential for peripheral nerve development. Overall, these
knockout studies underscore the importance of HER2 for proper cardiac development, as defects
can lead to severe malformations (39).
In order to complement the literature search, the results of the ReproTracker assay were
submitted to the authorities. In this assay, the differentiation of hiPSCs into cardiomyocytes,
hepatocytes, and neural rosettes was investigated by assessing morphology and biomarker gene
expression using qRT-PCR. The ReproTracker assay's validity was confirmed in every
differentiation experiment via exposure to a human teratogenic compound (thalidomide/retinoic
acid) and a non-teratogenic compound (saccharin). Thalidomide exposure significantly decreased
expression of cardiomyocyte-specific marker MYH6 and liver-specific markers FOXA2 and
AFP, disrupted cardiac contractions, and altered hepatocyte morphology. Retinoic acid exposure
led to a concentration-dependent decrease in neural rosette markers PAX6 and NESTIN and
affected their morphology. Saccharin had no impact on differentiated cells' morphology,
functionality, or biomarker gene expression, confirming ReproTracker's validity. Sevabertinib
did not significantly affect mesodermal marker expression but caused a concentration-dependent
decrease in MYH6, with significant changes starting at 1.0 μM, indicating disrupted
cardiomyocyte differentiation. This aligns with the loss of cardiac contraction ability at this
concentration. This result could be explained by the high expression of HER2 in cardiomyocytes.
Sevabertinib did not significantly affect the endodermal marker, FOXA2, but caused a decrease
in the hepatocyte-specific marker, AFP, starting at 0.2 μM, indicating disrupted hepatocyte
differentiation and morphology at 2.0 μM. It also showed a U-shaped response in the neural
rosette markers PAX6 and NESTIN, disrupting their morphology at 2.0 μM in one biological
replicate. Based on the assay criteria, ReproTracker identified sevabertinib as a teratogen,
disrupting proper stem cell differentiation into cardiomyocytes and hepatocytes.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 219972
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
Even though sevabertinib has a rather high selectivity with respect to the mutated EGFR
receptor, there is no relevant selectivity with respect to the mutated HER2 receptor and
consequently an interaction with wild-type HER2 cannot be avoided. Based on the
comprehensive evaluation of potential impact of EGFR and/or HER2 inhibition on embryo-fetal
development, it is clear that the existing data raises significant concerns regarding its potential
teratogenic effects. The absence of developmental toxicity in rat studies does not mitigate the
risks associated with EGFR and HER2 inhibition, particularly given their critical roles in
reproductive organ development and overall embryogenesis. The findings from knockout studies
and in vitro assays further suggest that sevabertinib may disrupt essential processes in fetal
growth and differentiation. Therefore, conducting another study in a non-rodent species on
embryo-fetal development is unlikely to alleviate these concerns, as the underlying mechanisms
of action and potential adverse effects have already been substantiated through various lines of
evidence.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s summary of the data, with additional pertinent
details. In the GLP-compliant preliminary EFD study (Study No. T105411-2, Report No.
B003097), sevabertinib was orally administered to pregnant females during organogenesis at
doses of 3, 7, and 11 mg/kg once daily from GD 6 to 17. Three animals at 11 mg/kg (0.5 times
the human exposure at the recommended dose of 20 mg BID based on AUC) were sacrificed in
moribund condition on GD 14 or GD 15 due to body weight loss up to -16% and severe clinical
signs including piloerection, cool to the touch, liquid feces, hunched posture, and/or thin
appearance. Similar clinical findings were observed in animals at 11 mg/kg that survived to
scheduled necropsy, in addition to observations of decreased general activity, labored respiration,
and partially closed eyes in individual animals. Decreased body weight was observed on GD 18
by -9% and -15% at 7 and 11 mg/kg, respectively (≥0.2 times the human exposure at the
recommended dose based on AUC), compared with concurrent controls, and only slightly
recovered following dosing cessation between GD 18 and GD 21 (-7% and -11%, respectively).
In addition, mean body weight gains during the dosing period at 7 and 11 mg/kg were
significantly lower than the controls (-33% and -56%, respectively), but were higher than the
controls (6% and 14%, respectively) following dosing cessation (GD 18 to GD 21). Overall food
consumption decreased by -17% and -30% at 7 and 11 mg/kg, respectively (≥0.2 times the
human exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC) from GD 6 through GD 18 compared
with controls, but fully recovered between GD 18 and GD 21. The Applicant was unable to
achieve higher exposures due to dose-limiting toxicities in pregnant rats, under the conditions
tested, and only limited variations were observed; however, they were close to the historical
control data (HCD) and not dose-dependent.
In the GLP-compliant pivotal EFD study (Study No. T106047-8, Report No. B003632),
sevabertinib was orally administered to pregnant females at doses of 1.5, 3, and 6 mg/kg once
daily from GD 6 to 17. There were no mortalities or clinical signs. During the dosing phase,
mean body weight gains at 1.5, 3, and 6 mg/kg (≥0.02 times the human exposure at the

Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0

6 Clinical Pharmacology
Executive Summary
The Applicant is seeking accelerated approval of sevabertinib for the treatment of adult patients
with advanced NSCLC whose tumors have HER2 (ERBB2) mutations and who have received
prior systemic therapy. The Applicant’s proposed recommended dosage is sevabertinib 20 mg
twice daily (BID) taken with food.
The clinical pharmacology review addressed the following key review issues:
Dosage Acceptability:
The proposed recommended dosage of sevabertinib 20 mg BID taken with food is supported by
available pharmacokinetics (PK), pharmacodynamics (PD), activity, and safety data from the
ongoing first-in-human (FIH) study 21607. The dosage of 20 mg BID showed a higher overall
response rate (ORR) in the targeted patients compared to the lower dosage of 10 mg BID (64%
vs 36%). Although 20 mg BID was also associated with higher rates of Grade ≥3 TEAEs and
TEAEs leading to dose modifications (i.e., dose reductions and interruptions), the overall safety
profile at this dose level is considered acceptable in the context of the observed activity. FDA
exposure-response (E-R) analyses identified a positive trend of ORR with higher exposure and a
significant positive E-R relationship for the safety endpoints (i.e., any Grade rash, any Grade and
Grade ≥ 3 diarrhea, AEs leading to dose modification). Further analysis using activity and safety
data of individual patients suggested that the dosage of 20 mg BID resulted in earlier response
and steeper tumor reduction as compared to 10 mg BID; diarrhea was generally manageable and
did not necessarily lead to dose reduction, regardless of severity and recurrence of diarrhea,
patient age, and dose level. Taken together, FDA agrees that the favorable benefit-risk profile
supports the proposed sevabertinib dosage of 20 mg BID in adult patients with NSCLC with
HER2 mutations.
Specific populations:
Population PK analyses found no clinically meaningful effects on the PK of sevabertinib for the
following covariates, including age (18 to 91 years), sex, race (27% White, 65% Asian), body
weight (29 to 155 kg), smoking status, renal impairment (eGFR 30 to < 90 mL/min), or mild
hepatic impairment (AST > ULN and total bilirubin ≤ ULN; or total bilirubin > 1 to 1.5x ULN
and any AST).
Hepatic Impairment: Patients with hepatic impairment may have serious risk of increased
exposure to sevabertinib as sevabertinib is eliminated primarily through hepatic metabolism. The
PK and safety of sevabertinib in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment have not
been evaluated. Given the rarity of the target patient population, a Post Marketing Requirement
(PMR) will be issued for a study to evaluate the potential serious risk of increased drug exposure
106
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5697816

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 219972
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
and determine a safe and appropriate dosage of sevabertinib in patients with moderate hepatic
impairment. The dosage recommendation in patients with severe hepatic impairment will be
determined based on the PK results in patients with moderate hepatic impairment or a subsequent
PMR study if needed.
Drug-drug interactions (DDIs):
Sevabertinib is a substrate for CYP3A4. Clinical DDI studies were conducted with itraconazole
(P-gp and strong CYP3A4 inhibitor) and carbamazepine (strong CYP3A4 inducer). The labeling
recommends avoidance of concomitant use of strong CYP3A4 inhibitors and dose reduction if
concomitant use cannot be avoided. Moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors may be used with safety
monitoring for increased adverse reactions. Given the significant changes in exposure of
sevabertinib following concomitant use of a strong CYP3A4 inducer, the labeling recommends
avoidance of concomitant use of strong or moderate CYP3A4 inducers.
Sevabertinib displays a net inhibitory effect on PK of CYP3A4 substrates. The clinical DDI
study conducted with midazolam (CYP3A4 substrate) showed approximately 2-fold increase in
midazolam exposure following concomitant use of sevabertinib 20 mg twice daily. The labeling
recommends avoid concomitant use of CYP3A4 substrates where minimal concentration changes
may lead to serious adverse reactions.
Sevabertinib is an inhibitor of P-gp. Based on clinical DDI results with dabigatran etexilate (P-gp
substrate), the labeling recommends referring to the Prescribing Information of P-gp substrates
where minimal concentration changes may lead to serious adverse reactions for recommended
dosage modification and/or monitoring.
The inhibitory effect of sevabertinib on CYP1A1 cannot be ruled out based on in vitro DDI
studies. However, given that CYP1A1 is not a typical enzyme with generally low expression, a
post-marketing study will not be required. Instead, the labeling recommends referring to the
Prescribing Information of CYP1A1 substrates when concomitant use of sevabertinib with these
substrates.
Based on the results of in vitro DDI studies, a PMR will be issued to conduct a clinical drug
interaction trial to assess the effect of sevabertinib on the PK of MATE1/2-K substrates and to
inform an appropriate drug interaction management strategy for coadministration with
sevabertinib.
QT Interval Prolongation:
Based on data from study 21607, sevabertinib does not demonstrate mean QTcF interval
prolongation ≥20 msec. The observed largest mean increase in the QTc interval was 3.9 msec
(upper confidence interval of 5.8 msec) at steady state Cmax after administration of 60 mg QD,
which covers the anticipated therapeutic sevabertinib Cmax by 2.2-fold.

Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
Based on the integrative analyses including PK-PD analyses/modeling, the clinical efficacy and
safety data presented, and exposure-response evaluations, a dose of 20 mg BID was selected as
the optimal dosage. While exposure of doses >20 mg BID was in the upper/flat part of the
exposure-response curve for ORR and maximal change in tumor size, more participants are
expected to reach >80% reduction in pEGFR over 24 hours with doses >20 mg BID and patients
might further benefit from these higher doses. On the other hand, exposure of doses >20 mg BID
showed a steep increase in the incidence of diarrhea Grade ≥3 and TEAEs leading to dose
modifications, which may limit the clinical application of these higher doses. While exposure of
doses <20 mg BID showed a decrease in AE incidence, as exposure was in the steep phase of the
exposure-response relationship for safety, exposure was also in the steep phase of the exposureresponse for efficacy, suggesting lower probability of efficacy (ORR and maximal change in
tumor size) compared to the 20 mg BID dose.
Simulations using a translational PK-PD model of pEGFR reduction after daily doses of 10 mg
to 80 mg, administered either QD or BID, based on the estimated PK variability in participants in
Study 21607, showed that BID dosing provides more sustained pEGFR reduction compared to
QD dosing of the same daily dose. As previously published preclinical and clinical data suggest
pEGFR levels should be reduced below a threshold of 80% reduction over 24 hours for clinical
efficacy, BID dosing was selected as the optimal dosing schedule.
Thus, data from the ongoing Study 21607, PK-PD, and exposure-response analyses collectively
support a favorable benefit/risk profile of the sevabertinib 20 mg BID regimen.
The Applicant’s Position:
The available data support the proposed dosing regimen of 20 mg BID for the treatment of
advanced NSCLC patients with HER2 activating mutations who have received prior systemic
therapy.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Based on PK, PD, and efficacy and safety results obtained from Study 21607, along with E-R
analyses, FDA agrees that the favorable benefit-risk profile supports the proposed dosage of
sevabertinib 20 mg BID in patients with advanced NSCLC harboring HER2 activating
mutations. Refer to Section 6.3.2.1 for details.
6.2.2.2.

Data:
The impact of intrinsic and extrinsic factors on the PK of sevabertinib was evaluated during
clinical development. The main findings are summarized below:
Intrinsic (and extrinsic) factors
The population PK analysis demonstrated that age (18 to 91 years), sex (120 male and
215 female), race (216 Asian, 91 White, 9 Black or African American, and 1 American Indian or

Alaska Native), geographic region (214 from Asia, 91 from Western Europe and Israel, 29 from
North America, 1 from South America, 36 from Japan, and 83 from Mainland China), body
weight (29.3 kg to 155 kg), mild or moderate renal impairment (146 mild and 25 moderate), mild

103 ex-smokers) do not affect the PK of sevabertinib (see Section 19.4.3.2). Therefore, no dose
adjustment is required for these intrinsic and extrinsic factors.
Extrinsic factors
Food: Light low fat or high fat/high calorie meals were found to reduce the Cmax of sevabertinib
(20 mg) by 27.8% and 55.7%, respectively, and resulted in a delay of time to reach Cmax (tmax)
from approximately 1 hour to 2 and 4 hours, and reduced overall exposure (AUC) by 16.1 and
27.6% in comparison with intake in fasted state. Comparing the effect of a high-fat, high-calorie
meal with the effect of a light, low-fat meal, AUC was decreased by 14% and Cmax was
decreased by 39%. ANOVA revealed that the LSM ratios [90% CI] of the Cmax and AUC of
sevabertinib for a low-fat meal vs fasted were 0.7225 [0.6295; 0.8293] and 0.8390 [0.7620;
0.9238], respectively. The LSM ratios [90% CI] of the Cmax and AUC of sevabertinib for a highfat/high-calorie meal vs fasted were 0.4432 [0.3861; 0.5087] and 0.7242 [0.6578; 0.7974],
respectively. The LSM ratios [90% CI] of the Cmax and AUC of sevabertinib for a high-fat/highcalorie meal vs a light, low-fat meal were 0.6134 [0.5344; 0.7041] and 0.8631 [0.7839; 0.9504],
respectively.
Acid-reducing agents: Sevabertinib is a lipophilic base showing strong pH-dependent solubility.
Despite the pH-dependent solubility, the proton pump inhibitor esomeprazole had no clinically
relevant effect on the exposure of sevabertinib after oral administration of 20 mg sevabertinib.
The LSM ratios [90% CI] were 0.9034 [0.7921; 1.0302] for AUC and 0.7074 [0.6128; 0.8165]
for Cmax.
Strong cytochrome P450 (CYP) 3A4 inhibitors: In vitro studies indicate that sevabertinib is
primarily metabolized by CYP3A4. Data from Study 22250 demonstrated that coadministration
of multiple oral doses (200 mg daily) of the strong CYP3A4 inhibitor (and P-gp inhibitor)
itraconazole significantly increased the PK of a single oral dose of 10 mg sevabertinib. The LSM
ratios [90% CI] were 2.2530 [1.9992; 2.5390] for AUC and 1.5973 [1.4701; 1.7356] for Cmax.
Moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors: Clinical data on moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor use are too limited to
evaluate the possible effect of moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor use on exposure, safety, and efficacy
of sevabertinib. On the basis of the observed effect of the strong CYP3A4 (and P-gp) inhibitor,
itraconazole, it cannot be excluded that sevabertinib exposure may increase when coadministered with moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors.
Weak CYP3A4 inhibitors: Exposure, safety, and efficacy data from Study 21607 did not show
clinically meaningful differences between participants with or without concomitant use of weak
CYP3A4 inhibitor(s). Sevabertinib may be given concomitantly with weak CYP3A4 inhibitors
without clinically relevant DDIs.

Strong CYP3A4 inducers: Study 22250 demonstrated that coadministration of multiple doses of
the strong CYP3A4 (and P-gp) inducer carbamazepine (titrated up from 100 mg BID to 300 mg
BID), significantly decreased the PK of a single oral 40 mg dose of sevabertinib. The LSM ratios
[90% CI] were 0.2116 [0.1825; 0.2453] for AUC and 0.4294 [0.3617; 0.5098] for Cmax.
Moderate CYP3A4 inducers: Sevabertinib exposure, safety, and efficacy were similar in
participants who used moderate CYP3A4 inducer(s), with/without P-gp inhibitor(s) compared to
the reference group of participants who took no inhibitors and/or inducers related to CYP3A4, Pgp, or BRCP during Cycle 1 in Study 21607. No adjustment of the sevabertinib dosage is needed
when sevabertinib is dosed concomitantly with a moderate CYP3A4 inducer.
Weak CYP3A4 inducers: Available data on weak CYP3A4 inducer use are too limited to
evaluate the possible effect of weak CYP3A4 inducer use on exposure, safety, and efficacy. As
the clinical data showed similar exposure, safety, and efficacy profiles for participants who used
moderate CYP3A4 inducers, no clinically relevant effect on exposure, safety, and efficacy of
concomitant use of weak CYP3A4 inducers is expected. No adjustment of the sevabertinib
dosage is needed when sevabertinib is dosed concomitantly with a weak CYP3A4 inducer.
Other inhibitors and inducers of transporters: In vitro studies suggest that sevabertinib is a
substrate of BCRP and P-gpand is not a substrate of OATP1B1 or OATP1B3.
No clinically relevant interactions with BCRP or P-gp inhibitors are expected due to high
permeability and limited unchanged excretion of sevabertinib. Clinical data from Study 21607
confirmed that sevabertinib exposure, efficacy, and safety profiles were similar in participants
with or without concomitant use of BCRP inhibitors. The number of participants who took P-gp
inhibitors, BCRP inducers, or P-gp inducers in Study 21607 was too low to make inferences on
possible differences in exposure, safety and efficacy.
The Applicant’s Position:
Therapeutic individualization is not required for the intrinsic (and extrinsic) factors of age, sex,
race, geographical region, body weight, mild hepatic impairment, mild or moderate renal
impairment, and smoking status.
To reduce the risk of gastrointestinal AEs, sevabertinib should be taken with food. The type of
meal (high-fat, high-calorie or light, low-calorie) is not relevant.
Sevabertinib may be given concomitantly with acid-reducing agents.

is not recommended. If concomitant use cannot be avoided, the current sevabertinib TDD
should be reduced by 50%. For patients taking a current total daily dose of 10 mg (one

ADME: After single oral administration of 20 mg sevabertinib to participants with NSCLC,
together with a light meal, maximum plasma concentrations were generally reached
approximately 2 hours (range: 0.5–8.2 hours) after administration (tmax). A tmax of 1.5 hours (CV:
33.7%) was estimated based on the population PK model. Geometric mean apparent volume of
distribution (Vz/F) was 28.1 L (CV: 42.2%). The apparent volume of the central compartment
(Vc/F) was estimated to be 30 L (CV: 31%) based on all data using the population PK model.
Sevabertinib is bound to human plasma proteins at 95.3%. The blood-to-plasma ratio is 0.61,
indicating that sevabertinib is more bound to plasma proteins than to blood cells. In vitro studies
suggest that sevabertinib is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP and is not a substrate of OATP1B1 or
OATP1B3.
In the human mass balance Study 22249 sevabertinib and the major metabolite M1 were the
main circulating moieties in plasma, constituting 59.3% and 38.5% of the total radioactivity,
respectively. M1 has less potent cellular activity in most of the evaluated HER2- and EGFRmutant variants. M1 is also bound to plasma proteins to a larger extent (98.1% vs 95.3%)
compared with the parent compound. Based on systemic exposure, relative potency, and PK
properties, M1 is unlikely to significantly contribute to the clinical activity of sevabertinib.
The geometric mean apparent clearance of sevabertinib is 3.6 L/hour (CV: 55.2%,). The
population PK analysis estimated a typical CL/F of 3.1 L/h (CV: 38%). The half-life is

94.1% of the administered dose, with 83.6% of the radioactivity excreted via feces and 10.4%
via urine. Only 14.0% of the dose was excreted in feces as unchanged sevabertinib, and
approximately 70% of the dose was excreted as metabolites. Excretion of unchanged
sevabertinib in urine was 1.34% of the administered radiolabeled dose and approximately 9%
was excreted as metabolites.
Dose- and time dependencies: Dose-proportional increase in exposure was observed following
administration of sevabertinib as an LSF in the doses of 10, 20, and 40 mg, and as tablet(s) in
TDDs of 20, 40, 60, and 80 mg. the population PK analysis confirmed that sevabertinib PK is
linear over the evaluated TDD range of 10 mg to 80 mg.
Steady state exposure is achieved within approximately 3 days of BID dosing and accumulation
is low (1.65) and as expected, based on the half-life and dosing interval.

Time-dependent PK of sevabertinib cannot be excluded based on the population PK analysis of
Study 21607, but the identified decrease in PK remains within the bioequivalence range and is
not considered clinically relevant.
Inter-individual variability: The inter-subject variability at steady state for sevabertinib exposure
[AUC(0–12)] was 50.2%, following administration of 20 mg BID sevabertinib in Study 21607.
This is consistent with the inter-subject variability in predicted exposure [AUC(0-24)ss] of 51%
in the population PK analysis.
Pharmacokinetics in target population: Sevabertinib exposure in participants with advanced
NSCLC harboring HER2 or EGFR mutations was approximately 2 times the exposure in healthy
participants based on the observed range of systemic exposure.
Effect on QTc: The mean change from baseline in QTcF interval was well below 10 ms, and the
upper limit of the 90% CI of the predicted baseline corrected QTcF (ΔQTcF) remained well
below the threshold of regulatory concern of a ΔQTcF of 20 ms, at doses up to 2 times the
recommended TDD.
The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical pharmacology of sevabertinib has been adequately assessed via in vitro studies,
dedicated clinical studies, and model-based population PK and exposure-response analyses.

Pharmacology
Mechanism of Action

Administration, formulation
type, strengths
QT Prolongation

Sevabertinib is a kinase inhibitor of human epidermal growth
factor receptor 2 (HER2). It also exhibits activity against
epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR). In vitro,
sevabertinib inhibited the proliferation of tumor cells
expressing wild-type HER2 and HER2 mutations. In vivo,
sevabertinib demonstrated antitumor activity in subcutaneous
mouse xenograft models derived from human NSCLC tumors
harboring an activating HER2 exon 20 mutation.
The to-be-marketed sevabertinib formulation is tablets in
strength of 10 mg. The recommended dosage is 20 mg
administered orally BID with food.
At 2 times the maximum recommended dose, a mean increase
in the QTc interval >20 ms was not observed.

10 mg to 60 mg (QD), 20 mg to 40 mg (BID)

Excretion

DDI potentials

Sevabertinib Cmax decreases 56% and AUC decreases 28%
with a high-fat meal (1000 calories, 50% fat) in healthy
subjects. No clinically significant differences in sevabertinib
pharmacokinetics were observed following administration of
a low-fat meal (400 calories, 25% fat), as compared to a highfat meal or overnight fasting.

After a single oral dose of radiolabeled sevabertinib 40 mg to

observed when used concomitantly with esomeprazole
(proton pump inhibitor).
Clinical Pharmacology Questions

Data:

6.3.2.1 Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of
effectiveness?

Data from Study 21607 demonstrated that a sevabertinib dose of 20 mg BID was effective at
achieving clinically meaningful and durable responses in advanced NSCLC patients with HER2
activating mutations. Details regarding efficacy are provided in Section 8.1.2.
Exposure-response analyses for ORR were conducted based on all participants with an ORR
observation and exposure estimate in Study 21607 (n=326), and a sub-set of participants from
Groups D and D1 only (n=115). Mutational status showed a considerable and statistically
significant effect on ORR based on investigator and BICR assessments and based on all data,
with participants with HER2 ex20ins mutations (n=217) showing an approximate 3- to 4-fold
higher ORR compared with participants with other mutations (n=109; odds ratio based on
investigator assessment: 2.94, 95% CI: [1.73, 4.98], odds ratio based on BICR: 3.59, 95% CI:
[2.1, 6.14]). Exposure did not show a statistically significant (p<0.01) association with ORR
based on both investigator and BICR assessments. The exposure-response for participants with
HER2 activating mutations in Groups D and D1 also showed no statistically significant (p<0.01)
association between exposure and ORR based on both investigator and BICR assessments. The
effect of sevabertinib on ORR in participants with activating mutations was not affected by any
of the baseline covariates tested (age, sex, body weight, race, geographical region, serum
albumin, smoking status, ALP, LDH, neutrophil to lymphocyte ratio, ECOG status, AJCC
grading at initial diagnosis, tumor stage, tumor size, number of metastatic sites, brain/liver
metastases, time since diagnosis, previous lines of anti-cancer therapy, and prior
platinum/immunotherapy/trastuzumab deruxtecan/HER2 ex20ins-targeted therapy/EGFR
ex20ins-targeted therapy). Exposure-response analyses for BTR in participants with HER2
activating mutations in Groups D and D1 showed increasing BTR, i.e. a more pronounced
decrease in tumor size from baseline, with increasing exposure. None of the pre-selected baseline
covariates showed a statistically significant effect on BTR.
Overall, the results indicate that the efficacy benefit of sevabertinib was highest in the targeted
patient population, i.e. patients with advanced NSCLC whose tumors have activating HER2
mutations, and that the effect in these patients is consistent regardless of a variety of
demographic and clinical baseline characteristics.

Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
The Applicant’s Position:
Sevabertinib should be taken with food.
No clinically relevant effect of esomeprazole on the PK of sevabertinib was observed. Thus,
sevabertinib may be given concomitantly with acid-reducing agents (e.g. proton pump inhibitors,
H2-receptor antagonists, and locally acting antacids).
There are clinically relevant DDIs associated with sevabertinib. The recommended management
strategies are outlined below:
Study 22250 demonstrated that strong CYP3A4 inhibitors increase exposure of sevabertinib (see
Section 6.2.2.2). Concomitant use of strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (including, but not limited to,
clarithromycin, itraconazole, ketoconazole, cobicistat, lopinavir/ritonavir, saquinavir/ritonavir,
and grapefruit or grapefruit juice) during treatment with sevabertinib is not recommended. If
concomitant use cannot be avoided, the current sevabertinib TDD should be reduced by 50%.
For patients taking a total daily dose of 10 mg (one 10 mg tablet once daily), sevabertinib should
be temporarily withheld until the strong CPY3A4 inhibitor is completed. After the CYP3A4
inhibitor has been discontinued for 3 to 5 elimination half-lives, sevabertinib should be resumed
at the dose taken prior to initiating the inhibitor.
As sevabertinib exposure may be increased when co-administered with moderate CYP3A4
inhibitors, it is recommended to closely monitor patients for adverse reactions. Sevabertinib may
be given concomitantly with weak CYP3A4 inhibitors without clinically relevant DDIs.
Strong CYP3A4 inducers decrease the exposure of sevabertinib based on the results of Study
22250 (see Section 6.2.2.2). Use of strong CYP3A4 inducers (including, but not limited to,
carbamazepine, phenytoin, rifabutin, rifampicin, and St. John’s Wort) during treatment with
sevabertinib is not recommended, since decreased sevabertinib plasma concentrations are
expected to result in reduced efficacy. Selection of an alternate concomitant medicinal product,
with no or less potential to induce CYP3A4 should be considered.
No adjustment of the sevabertinib dosage is required when sevabertinib is dosed concomitantly
with a weak or moderate CYP3A4 inducer.
Study 22251 demonstrated that sevabertinib is a weak CYP3A4 inhibitor and that concomitant
use of sevabertinib may increase the plasma concentrations of sensitive CYP3A4 substrates.
Therefore, the related recommendation in the product information of sensitive CYP3A4
substrates, with a narrow therapeutic window (including but not limited to alfentanil,
cyclosporine, dihydroergotamine, ergotamine, fentanyl, pimozide, quinidine, sirolimus, or
tacrolimus), should be followed when co-administered with sevabertinib.
Based on the results from Study 22252, sevabertinib is a P-gp inhibitor and may increase the
plasma concentrations of sensitive P-gp substrates. Therefore, related recommendation in the
product information of sensitive P-gp substrates, with a narrow therapeutic window (including
but not limited to digoxin), should be followed when co-administered with sevabertinib.
127
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5697816

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – NDA 219972
Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
the moderate CYP3A4 inducers would also significantly alter the exposure of sevabertinib. In
addition, there are no sufficient data over a wider dose range at this point to suggest efficacy
would maintain with reduced exposure. Therefore, FDA recommends avoid the concomitant use
of moderate CYP3A4 inducers as well.
FDA does not consider avoidance of concomitant use of BCRP substrates is necessary. Study
22252 demonstrated that rosuvastatin (BCRP substrate) AUC increased 1.2-fold and Cmax 1.4fold following concomitant use of HYRNUO. The limited exposure change of rosuvastatin
suggests that there is unlikely a clinically significant impact on other BCRP substrates which
usually exhibit lower degree of PK alternation compared to rosuvastatin
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment that sevabertinib is a strong inhibitor of CYP1A1 at
clinically relevant concentrations in vitro. According to FDA analysis, the inhibitory effect of
sevabertinib on CYP1A1 in vivo cannot be ruled out, i.e., the exposure change of CYP1A1
substrates is likely to be more than 3-fold. Therefore, FDA agrees with the Applicant to include
relevant DDI instructions in the label. However, a PMR clinical drug interaction trial to assess
the effect of sevabertinib on the PK of CYP1A1 substrates is not required given no available
PMR examples evaluating the impact on CYP1A1, the overall low expression levels of
CYP1A1, and the limited involvement of CYP1A1 in drug metabolism.
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment that sevabertinib is an inhibitor of MATE1/2-K at
clinically relevant concentrations in vitro. A PMR will be issued to conduct a clinical drug
interaction trial to assess the effect of sevabertinib on the PK of MATE1/2-K substrates and to
inform an appropriate drug interaction management strategy for coadministration with
sevabertinib.
Given the embryo-fetal toxicity of sevabertinib, FDA evaluated the potential impact of
sevabertinib on combined oral contraceptives (COCs) via induction effect on CYP enzymes.
Study 22251 demonstrated that midazolam (CYP3A4 substrate) AUC increased 2-fold and Cmax
increased 1.8-fold following concomitant use of HYRUNO, indicating a net inhibitory effect of
sevabertinib on CYP3A4, which suggests the CYP3A pathway is unlikely to cause efficacy
reduction of COCs. The potential induction effect of sevabertinib on other CYP enzymes (e.g.,
CYP2B6 and CYP2C19) that may also contribute to metabolism of COCs was evaluated by the
Applicant using two analytical models, i.e., relative induction score (RIS) method and
mechanistic static model (MSM). Despite uncertainties on accuracy of the model prediction,
FDA agrees with the low risk of efficacy reduction of COCs via drug interactions with
sevabertinib. In addition, majority of the female patients with the targeted indication (NSCLC)
are postmenopausal, which further reduces the risk. Therefore, FDA agrees that no specific
labeling recommendation for COCs is needed.

8 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
Study 21607 (SOHO-01)
The efficacy analyses in support of the proposed indication are based on data from Study 21607
to evaluate sevabertinib in participants with locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC harboring
EGFR and/or HER2 activating mutations (see Table 27). Study 21607 consists of Dose
Escalation, Backfill, Dose Expansion, and Dose Extension parts, with the latter representing the
Phase 2 part of the study (Figure 5).
Populations supporting efficacy
The efficacy of sevabertinib is presented for the following populations of patients with advanced
NSCLC harboring HER2 activating mutations (including ex20ins):
•

Primary efficacy population is Group D (N=81), which includes participants who have
received prior systemic therapy but were naïve to HER2 ex20ins-targeted therapy.
Participants who had prior treatment with HER2 ex20ins-targeted therapy for ≤2 months
and stopped treatment due to reasons other than progressive disease were also eligible.

•

Supportive efficacy data are provided by Group E (N=55), which includes participants
who have received prior systemic therapy and had documented progression to HER2targeted ADCs. Prior treatment with HER2 ex20ins-TKIs for ≤2 months was
exclusionary unless treatment lasted for ≤2 months and was stopped due to reasons other
than progressive disease.

These populations include participants from DE/BF, Dose Expansion, and Dose Extension parts
of the study who received sevabertinib at the 20 mg BID dose and who met the eligibility criteria
for Groups D and E. Group D included 37 participants from the Dose Extension, 34 from Dose
Expansion, and 10 from the DE/BF part of the study. In Group E, 21 participants were from
Dose Extension, 31 were from the Dose Expansion, and 3 were from the DE/BF part of the
study.
As follow-up was longer and the data are more mature in the DE/BF and Dose Expansion parts
compared to the Dose Extension part, efficacy data (ORR, DOR, DCR, and PFS), based on
response assessments by BICR, are also presented by study part:
•

DE/BF/Expansion (N=44 in Group D and N=34 in Group E).

•

Extension (N=37 in Group D and N=21 in Group E).

Trial Design
The Applicant’s Description:
Basic study design
The study design is shown in Figure 5. The primary objectives of the Escalation, Backfill and
Expansion parts of the study were to determine the safety, tolerability, and the MTD or MAD,
and to characterize the PK of sevabertinib. The primary objective of the Extension part was to
determine the efficacy of sevabertinib at the RP2D based on confirmed ORR per RECIST v1.1,
as assessed by BICR, as the primary endpoint.
The study started enrolling participants on 25 OCT 2021 and is being conducted in 14
countries/regions, including the US. At the time of the database cut-off date of 14 OCT 2024,
enrollment in the DE/BF and Expansion parts was complete, while enrollment in the Dose
Extension part (Group F only) was still ongoing. The Extension Group F was not opened for
enrollment in the US.

(as of 14 OCT 2024 database cutoff).
Study treatments
Sevabertinib was administered as a liquid formulation or as coated tablets in 21-days cycles of
continuous dosing. The liquid formulation was used in 3 cohorts in the DE/BF part of the study
(Figure 5).
Dose selection
The sevabertinib doses administered across 8 dosing regimens (QD and BID schedules) in the
DE/BF cohorts are shown in Figure 5. Based on the totality of the available data at the time of
PK/PD analysis from the ongoing Study 21607, 20 mg BID was considered the recommended
dose of sevabertinib for the Expansion part, with an optimal balance of highest predicted benefit
to patients while maintaining an acceptable safety profile. Further details are provided in Section
6.3.
Assignment to treatment
Randomization was not applicable in this open-label, single-arm study. At the time this study
was initiated, there was no approved targeted therapy for patients with metastatic NSCLC with
HER2 (ERBB2) mutations who have received a prior systemic therapy.
Dose modification, discontinuation
Specific rules for dose modifications and discontinuation are outlined in the study protocol
(Section 6.2). If a participant experienced a CTCAE Grade 3 or 4 and/or unacceptable toxicity
including a DLT, not attributable to the disease or disease-related processes under investigation,
dosing was interrupted until the toxicity resolved or recovered to ≤ Grade 2 within 21 days of
onset and could be then resumed at the same dose or at a lower dose according to the rules for
dose modifications.
If toxicity did not resolve to ≤ Grade 2 after 21 days, then the participant could be withdrawn
from treatment and observed until resolution of the toxicity. If a toxicity required dose
reduction and the participant was already on the starting dose of Dose Escalation (10 mg), study
treatment was discontinued. A maximum of 2 dose level reductions was allowed, and reescalation was not allowed.
Administrative structure
An independent SMC was established to ensure the ongoing safety of the study participants.
The SMC closely monitored the safety and tolerability throughout the Expansion and Extension
parts of the study and made recommendations.
As of 14 OCT 2024, there were 3 SMC meetings for safety, all with the recommendation to
continue the study without modification.

Procedures and schedule
Screening period: Screening tests were performed up to 28 days prior to receiving the first dose
of the study drug.
Treatment period: Participants received study treatment until disease progression per RECIST
v1.1 by investigator assessment, unacceptable toxicity or until any other withdrawal were met.
Participants were evaluated for efficacy (tumor assessments by CT/MRI) every 6 weeks (±7
days) for the first 36 weeks and then every 9 weeks (± 7 days) thereafter. The treatment period
ended with the EoT visit or, if the EoT visit was not performed, at the time of last intake of study
medication.
Active follow-up period: Following the completion of the EoT visit, this period comprised a
Safety follow-up visit and Imaging follow-up visits (for participants who discontinue study
treatment without disease progression). Participants who discontinued study treatment due to
disease progression, per RECIST v1.1 as by Investigator assessment, were to terminate the
Active follow-up period after the Safety follow-up visit. During the Imaging follow-up visits,
tumor assessments by CT/MRI, brain MRI/CT and/or bone scans (when applicable) were
acquired.
Long-term (survival) follow-up period: A Long-term follow-up period started after the Safety
follow-up visit/contact or after the last Imaging follow-up visit (whichever was last). Participants
were contacted every 3 months (±14 days) to determine survival.
The full schedule of evaluations is provided in Section 1.2 of the study protocol.
Concurrent medications
Prohibited and permitted medications are described in Section 6.7 of the study protocol.
Treatment compliance
Study drug administration was to be documented in the source documents and in the CRF. When
participants self-administered study treatments at home, compliance with study treatment was to
be assessed at each visit. Deviation(s) from the prescribed dosage regimen were to be recorded in
the CRF.
Rescue medication
Throughout the course of the study, participants were to receive appropriate supportive care
measures as deemed necessary by the treating Investigator for management of symptoms and
underlying medical conditions, which may develop during the study.
Patient completion, discontinuation, or withdrawal

exposure are provided for the SAF, which was identical to the FAS.
Data imputation was applied for instances of partial missing dates for AEs or concomitant
medication, and missing parts of either the censoring date or the event date for the time-to event
endpoints (only if the day was missing), where missing or partial dates were imputed as detailed
in the SAP.
Determination of sample size
For Group D, a true ORR of ≥ 50% was hypothesized. An approximate sample size of 70
participants including participants from Dose Expansion and Dose Extension, with a minimum
of 30 responders, was estimated to provide 90.6% power to achieve a lower bound of a 2-sided
95% exact binomial CI for the estimated ORR that exceeds 30%. For Group E, a true ORR of
≥ 40% was hypothesized. An approximate sample size of 51 participants, including participants
from Dose Expansion and Dose Extension, with a minimum of 17 responders, was estimated to
provide 86.8% power to achieve a lower bound of a 2-sided 95% exact binomial CI for the
estimated ORR that exceeds 20%.
Analysis of the efficacy endpoints
The ORR was assessed by both BICR (primary efficacy endpoint) and by the investigator
(secondary efficacy endpoint). The exact 2-sided 95% CIs for the ORR were calculated based on
binomial distribution. Sensitivity analyses of ORR by BICR were performed similarly for the
EFS. The secondary efficacy endpoints, DOR and PFS by investigator assessment and BICR,
and OS were evaluated using Kaplan-Meier methods, and the median value and corresponding
95% CIs for each were calculated. In addition, analysis of time to response was performed as an
additional efficacy endpoint by both BICR and by the investigator using Kaplan-Meier methods.
RECIST v1.1 criteria were used for response and progression assessments.
Exploratory subgroup analyses of ORR were performed for the following prespecified subgroups
of the FAS: demographics (age, sex, and race), ECOG PS at baseline, geographical region,
smoking status, histology (adenocarcinoma yes vs. no; squamous vs. non-squamous), prior
surgery (pneumonectomy), number of prior systemic anti-cancer therapy regimens,
presence/absence of brain metastases at baseline, prior platinum therapy, prior anti-PD(L)1
treatment, and HER2 TKD mutations status at baseline.
Safety analyses are described in Section 8.2.
Interim Analysis
During the Dose Expansion and Dose Extension parts, no formal interim analyses of the data
were planned, and futility was to be assessed independently for each Expansion cohort when
approximately 14-17 participants evaluable for efficacy were observed. Based on futility
assessments in the Expansion part, Groups D and E were prioritized in the Extension part. 2
primary analyses were planned to be performed when Group D and Group E participants
completed the Dose Extension part, respectively.

The Applicant’s Position:
Study 21607 was conducted in 14 countries/regions globally, with most participants enrolled
from Asia and Western Europe.
The 81 participants in the primary efficacy population, Group D, had a median age of 60.0 years
and the participants were predominantly female (61.7%) and Asian (70.4%). In Group E, the
median age was 65.0 years, and the majority of participants were female (65.5%) and 58.2%
were Asian.
As of the database cut-off date, 30 participants (8.4%) out of the 357 treated participants were
from the US. The primary efficacy population, Group D, included 1 participant (1.2%) from the
US. Despite the limited representation of US participants in Study 21607, the inclusion of
diverse populations from Asia and Western Europe may provide valuable insights into the
efficacy and safety of the treatment, which could be relevant for the multiethnic US population
with the disease, especially considering the global nature of disease presentation and treatment
response.

Psycholeptics (35.8% and 50.9%)

Concomitant medications were predominantly used for the management of comorbidities and
toxicity. Overall, the concomitant treatments administered were representative of those
commonly prescribed for patients with locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC harboring EGFR
and/or HER2 mutations, and/or for other illnesses commonly encountered in populations of a
similar age.
Rescue medication
Not applicable.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. As above the most common concomitant medication
was an anti-diarrheal. There are no concomitant medications that are known to affect the efficacy
results. FDA notes that the category listed above for antibiotics and chemotherapeutics for
dermatologic use refers to ATC subclass D06B containing compounds such as sulfonamides,
anti-viral, and metronidazole for roasacea.

Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint (Including Sensitivity Analyses)
Data:
An analysis of ORR in Groups D and E was performed using data from a database cut-off date of
28 MAR 2025. This database cutoff was determined as the date all ongoing responders in
Groups D and E had reached at least six months of follow-up on DOR. The results are
summarized below, using an extra section to present the updated best overall responses by BICR
for Groups D and E for the FAS. Results as of the original cut-off date of 14 OCT 2024 are
available in the section below the updated data.

Updated results as of 28 MAR 2025:
Primary analysis of ORR – as of 28 MAR 2025:
In the primary efficacy population, Group D, the confirmed ORR per RECIST v1.1 by BICR was
64.2% (95% CI: [52.8%, 74.6%]) among the 81 participants in the FAS, with the lower limit of
the 95% CI exceeding the predefined 30% limit (SAP version 2.0, Module 5.3.5.2,
Report B003047, Section 10.1.9). A total of 52 participants achieved a response, including
2 participants (2.5%) with CR and 50 (61.7%) with PR as the best response (37).

Sensitivity analysis of ORR by BICR – as of 28 MAR 2025
A total of 80 participants were evaluable, as per EFS, and were included in the sensitivity
analysis. The ORR was 65.0% (95% CI: [53.5%, 75.3%]) by BICR and 63.8% (95% CI: [52.2%,
74.2%]) by the investigator assessment, which were consistent with the results of the main ORR
analysis in the FAS (Module 5.3.5.2, B004166, Tables 8.2.1/1 and 8.2.1/2 [Datasets ADRS and ADSL]).
Best percent change from baseline in the size of the target lesions – as of 28 MAR 2025
The best percent change from baseline in the size of the target lesions based on assessment by
BICR is presented for Group D in Figure 6. Of the 81 participants in Group D (FAS), the best
change could be determined for 79 participants. A reduction in the target lesions from baseline
was observed for 77 participants (95.1%), with 16 (19.8%) having a ≥70% reduction.
2 participants did not have a complete post-baseline assessment and are not displayed in the
waterfall plot (Module 5.3.5.2, B004166, Table 8.2.1/8 [Datasets ADTR and ADSL]).

Subgroup analyses of ORR – as of 28 MAR 2025
The ORR results in Group D were consistent and >57% across the pre-specified subgroups,
based on demographics, baseline cancer characteristics, and prior treatments (Figure 7). The
exact binomial 95% CI lower limits about the estimated ORR exceeded 30% for all subgroups.

ADSL]).

Primary analysis of ORR – as of 14 OCT 2024:
In the primary efficacy population, Group D, the confirmed ORR per RECIST v1.1 by BICR was

when compared to the Dose Extension part, the ORR is also presented by study part:

•

In Extension (N=37), the ORR by BICR was 64.9% (95% CI: [47.5, 79.8]).

Sensitivity analysis of ORR by BICR – as of 14 OCT 2024
A total of 80 participants were evaluable, as per EFS, and were included in the sensitivity
analysis. The ORR was 61.3% (95% CI: [49.7, 71.9]) by BICR and 60.0% (95% CI: [48.4, 70.8])
by the investigator assessment, which were consistent with the results of the main ORR analysis
in the FAS (Module 5.3.5.2, B003047, Table 8.2.1/3 [Datasets ADSL and ADTR]).
The best percent change from baseline in the size of the target lesions based on response
assessment by BICR is presented for Group D in Figure 8. Of the 81 participants in Group D
(FAS), the best change could be determined for 79 participants (97.5%). A reduction in the target
lesions from baseline was observed for 77 participants (95.1%), with 13 participants (16.0%)
having a ≥70% reduction. 2 participants did not have a complete post-baseline assessment and
are not displayed in the waterfall plot (Module 5.3.5.2, B003047, Table 8.2.1/3s1 [Datasets ADSL, ADTR]).

Results of exposure-response analyses confirm that the efficacy benefit of sevabertinib was
highest in the targeted patient population, i.e. patients with advanced NSCLC whose tumors have
activating HER2 mutations (see Section 6.3.2.1), regardless of baseline demographic and clinical
characteristics.
Based on data from Study 21607, no clinically meaningful differences are anticipated in the
efficacy of sevabertinib in the US vs. non-US NSCLC patients with HER2 activating mutations.
Supportive efficacy results in Group E – as of 14 OCT 2024
In the supportive efficacy population, Group E, the confirmed ORR by BICR was 34.5%
(95% CI: [22.2, 48.6]) among the 55 participants in the FAS. A total of 19 participants achieved
a response, including 3 participants (5.5%) with a CR and 16 participants (29.1%) who had a PR
as their best response (Table 40).

The Applicant’s Position:
In terms of efficacy, sevabertinib demonstrated clinically meaningful activity in the treatment of
advanced NSCLC patients with HER2 activating mutations, who have received prior systemic
therapy.

ORR – as of 28 MAR 2025
Based on the investigator assessment, 51 of the 81 participants in Group D (FAS) achieved a
confirmed response, resulting in an ORR of 63.0% (95% CI: [51.5%, 73.4%]). The responders
included 1 participant (1.2%) with a CR and 50 (61.7%) with a PR (Table 42).
Considering the responders and the non-responders, the concordance rate between BICR and
investigator assessments, with an agreement rate of 69.14% for Group D, suggests reliable data
consistency (Module 5.3.5.2, B004166, Table 8.2.1/10 [Datasets ADRS and ADSL]).

DOR in Group D – 28 MAR 2025
Updated data Confirmed Responders:
At the database cutoff date of 28 MAR 2025, the median DOR by BICR for participants with
confirmed CR or PR in Group D by 14 OCT 2024 (N=49) was 9.2 months (95% CI: [5.8, 15.0])
in the FAS (Table 43), with a median follow-up time of 6.8 months from initial radiological
response (Module 5.3.5.2, B004166, Table 8.2.2/5 [Datasets ADTTE and ADSL]).
Of the 49 responders, 27 participants (55.1%) had an observed DOR of ≥6 months,
12 (24.5%) had an observed DOR of ≥9 months, and 9 (18.4%) had an observed DOR of
≥12 months (Module 5.3.5.2, B004166, Table 8.2.2/8 [Datasets ADTTE and ADSL]).
Based on the Kaplan-Meier method, 64.1% of responders were estimated to have a DOR of
≥6 months after initial radiological response, and 51.0% and 42.5% of the responders were
estimated to be still responding at 9 months and 12 months, respectively (Table 43).
Of the responders, 21 participants (42.9%) were censored (Table 43), with 76.2% of censored
participants having no documented radiological PD or death up to the date of last tumor

All Responders:
The median DOR for all participants with confirmed CR or PR in Group D (N=52) by BICR as

(Table 43), with a median follow-up time of 6.5 months from initial radiological response
(Module 5.3.5.2, B004166, Table 8.2.3/5 [Datasets ADTTE and ADSL]).
Of the 52 responders, 28 participants (53.8%) had an observed DOR of ≥6 months, 13 (25.0%)
had an observed DOR of ≥9 months, and 9 participants (17.3%) had an observed DOR of
≥12 months as of the database cut-off date (Module 5.3.5.2, B004166, Table 8.2.3/8 [Datasets ADTTE and
ADSL]).
Based on the Kaplan-Meier method, 65.6% of responders were estimated to have a DOR of
≥6 months after initial radiological response, and 52.8% and 44.0% of the responders were
estimated to be still responding at 9 months and 12 months, respectively (Table 43).
Of the responders, 24 participants (46.2%) were censored (Table 43), with 79.2% of censored
participants having no documented radiological PD or death up to the date of last tumor
assessment (Module 5.3.5.2, B004166, Table 8.2.3/9 [Datasets ADTTE and ADSL]).
The median DOR based on investigator assessment was 8.2 months (95% CI: [5.5, 12.2]),
which was consistent with the BICR assessment of response (9.2 months (95% CI: [6.3, 15.0])
(Table 43).

Swimmer plots in Figure 13.

ORR – as of 14 OCT 2024
Based on the investigator assessment, 48 of the 81 participants in Group D (FAS) achieved a
confirmed response, resulting in an ORR of 59.3% (95% CI: [47.8, 70.1]). The responders
included 1 participant (1.2%) with a CR and 47 (58.0%) with a PR (Table 45).
Considering the responders and the non-responders, the concordance rate between BICR and
investigator assessments, with an agreement rate of 71.60% for Group D, suggests reliable data
consistency (Module 5.3.5.2, B003047, Table 8.2.1/12 [Datasets ADSL and ADRS]).
DCR – as of 14 OCT 2024
The DCR by BICR for Group D was 81.5% (95% CI: [71.3, 89.2]) (Table 39). The DCR based
on the investigator assessment was 84.0% (95% CI: [74.1, 91.2%], which was consistent with the
result by BICR (Table 45).

Of the 49 responders, 13 participants (26.5%) had an observed DOR of ≥6 months, 10

estimated to be still responding at 9 months and 12 months, respectively.
Of the responders, 29 participants (59.2%) were censored, with 86.2% of censored participants
having no documented radiological PD or death up to the date of last tumor assessment (Table
46). Therefore, the median DOR is subject to change with longer follow-up of the responders
who showed no evidence of radiological PD or death as of the database cut-off date of 14 OCT
2024. When analyzed by study part, the median DOR was 9.2 months for the DE/BF/Expansion
part, at a median follow-up time of 6.3 months from initial radiological response compared to the
median DOR of 5.2 months for the Extension part, with a median follow-up time of 4.1 months
(Module 5.3.5.2, B003047, Tables 8.2.2/3, 8.2.2/3s1, 8.2.2/6 and 8.2.2/7 [Datasets ADSL and ADTTE]).

plot in Figure 15. The median DOR, based on investigator assessment, was 6.8 months (95% CI:
[5.2, not estimable]), which was consistent with the BICR assessment of response (6.8 months
(95% CI: [5.2, 15.0]) (Table 46).
TTR, PFS, and OS – as of 14 OCT 2024
Responses were achieved in a relatively short time in Group D; the median TTR of
participants with confirmed CR or PR by BICR was 1.4 months (95% CI: [1.3, 1.4]), with a
range of 1.1 months to 4.2 months. These results were consistent with the TTR assessed by the
investigator (Table 47).
The median PFS in Group D was 7.1 months (95% CI: [6.5, 14.7]) by BICR and 7.5 months
(95% CI: [6.6, 12.4]) by investigator assessment. As of the database cut-off date for the efficacy
analyses, follow-up of participants was still limited for a mature OS analysis. The median OS
was not reached in Group D, with 62 participants (76.5%) censored. The range in OS (including
censored values) was 0.9 months to 24.3 months.

The Applicant’s Position:
Treatment with sevabertinib at a dose of 20 mg BID resulted in rapid, deep, and durable
responses in advanced NSCLC patients with HER2 activating mutations, who had received prior
systemic therapy but were naïve to HER2 ex20ins-targeted therapy.
Updated results as of 28 MAR 2025:
At the data cut-off date of 28 MAR 2025, the updated ORR and DOR results from Group D
indicate that treatment with sevabertinib at 20 mg BID led to rapid, deep, and durable response in
participants with advanced NSCLC harboring HER2 activating mutations, who had received
prior systemic therapy but were naive to HER2 ex20ins-targeted therapy. In addition, the results
from Group E, consisting of participants who had received prior systemic therapy and had
documented progression to HER2-targeted ADC, further support the efficacy observed in
Group D.
The increased ORR and median DOR in the updated analysis highlights the potential clinical
benefit of sevabertinib for participants with HER2-mutant NSCLC who have received a prior
systemic therapy.
Group D, N=81 (FAS)
•

For the subset of 49 participants with a confirmed response by 14 OCT 2024, the median
DOR by BICR was 9.2 months (95% CI: [5.8, 15.0]), indicating the durable and mature
follow-up data in Group D.

•

For all 52 responders as of 28 MAR 2025, the confirmed ORR by BICR was 64.2% (95%
CI: [52.8%, 74.6%]), exceeding the ORR of 60.5% reported for participants at the
14 OCT 2024 database cut-off date.
The median DOR by BICR of 9.2 months (95% CI: [6.3, 15.0]) of all responders was
consistent with the results for the previous responders.

Group E, N=55 (FAS)
•

For all 21 responders as of 28 MAR 2025, the confirmed ORR by BICR was 38.2%
(95% CI: [25.4%, 52.3%]).

•

The updated median DOR by BICR was 7.0 months (95% CI: [5.6, not estimable]) for
both the 19 responders as of 14 OCT 2024 and all 21 responders.

•

The observed ORR and the preliminary durability of response further support the findings
from Group D.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The primary efficacy data for this application are based on a revised population of 70 patients
with non-squamous NSCLC with activating HER2 TKD mutations who were naïve to HER2targeted therapy (Group D), and 52 patients who had received a prior HER2 ADC (Group E).
The efficacy results demonstrated clinically meaningful and durable antitumor activity in this
population, with a confirmed ORR of 71% (95% CI: 59, 82) as assessed by BICR in Group D.
Among the 50 responders in Group D, the median DOR was 9.2 months (95% CI: 6.3, 15.0) with
a median follow-up of 9.0 months. At least 54% of the responders in Group D had a confirmed
response maintained for 6 months or longer. Although the DOR estimate was sensitive to
different censoring scenarios, the overall durability of response demonstrated that sevabertinib
provides a potential clinical benefit in this patient population with limited therapeutic options.
Among patients in Group E (n=52), the confirmed ORR was 38% (95% CI: 25, 53) with median
DOR of 7.0 months (95% CI: 5.6, NR) and at least 60% of the responders in Group E had a
confirmed response maintained for 6 months or longer. The evidence of efficacy was consistent
across prespecified subgroups.

Review of Safety
Data:
The main safety analyses to support the proposed indication are based on the data from
Study 21607 (SOHO-01), a non-randomized, open-label, single-arm, multicenter, Phase 1/2
study in participants with advanced NSCLC harboring activating mutations in HER2 and/or
EGFR. The key safety findings from Study 21607 are presented in the following sections. An
integrated analysis of safety was not performed, since no data from other clinical studies in
cancer patients are available for this submission. All safety analyses were performed using the
SAF, which included all participants who received at least 1 dose of sevabertinib. As of the
database cut-off date, a total of 357 participants had started treatment and were included in the
safety analyses.
For completeness, supportive safety data from 5 clinical pharmacology studies (Studies 22249,
22250, 22251, 22252, and 2253), conducted in 89 healthy participants to evaluate sevabertinib
for DDI potential, mass balance, and the effect of food, is provided in the NDA Module 2.7.4.
The primary safety analysis population is Group D (N=81), which includes participants with

from the Phase 1/2 study show that sevabertinib was generally well tolerated at the 20 mg BID
dose, and the most common drug-related TEAEs were of low grade, reversible, monitorable, and
manageable.
The FDA’s Assessment:

adequate assessment of the safety profile. All FDA independent safety analyses were performed
using the datasets submitted with the original application (with data cut-off [DCO] date of
October 14, 2024).

with unresectable or metastatic NSCLC with HER2 mutations who have received prior systemic

safety population, used to inform Section 5 of labeling, includes all patients in SOHO-01 with
NSCLC treated at a dose of 20 mg BID (n=268).

Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
Data:
The safety profile of sevabertinib was assessed by standard approaches and measures:
monitoring of AEs, laboratory evaluations (hematology, general chemistry, and urinalysis),
measurement of vital signs, and recording of 12-lead ECGs. In addition, this safety summary
includes subgroup analyses by age (<65 years vs. ≥65 years), sex (male vs. female), race (Asian
vs. White vs. Other), and geographical region (Asia vs. non-Asia).
The primary safety population comprises of 81 participants who qualified for Group D in
Study 21607 and were treated with sevabertinib at 20 mg BID (RP2D). In addition, safety data
are provided from 268 participants receiving the 20 mg BID dose (Whole Study 20 mg BID
population) and from 357 participants who received at least 1 dose of sevabertinib at any dose
level in Study 21607 (Whole Study SAF) (Section 8.2).
The Applicant’s Position:
The safety data from 81 participants in the target indication population, together with data from
268 participants treated with the RP2D and 357 participants treated with any dose of
sevabertinib, are sufficient to establish the safety profile for the proposed indication of
sevabertinib as a treatment for patients with advanced NSCLC whose tumors have activating
HER2 (ERBB2) mutations and who have received a prior systemic therapy. As agreed with the
FDA, the Applicant will use the primary safety population to inform the Adverse Drug Reactions
section of the USPI and the pooled safety population (20 mg BID) to inform the Warnings and
Precautions section of the USPI.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that the safety analyses presented were comprehensive and allowed sufficient review
of the safety data; however, FDA reviews all AEs regardless of attribution given the challenges
assessing relatedness in the context of a single-arm trial. Therefore, FDA’s analyses report AEs
regardless of attribution.

Relevant characteristics of the safety population:
Data:
Demographics and the baseline cancer characteristics of the groups used for the safety analyses
are presented in Table 33 and Table 35, respectively.
The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, more female than male participants received sevabertinib in Study 21607, and the
majority of the participants were Asian across the safety populations (Table 33). The median age
ranged from 60.0 years in Group D to 62.0 years in the Whole Study 20 mg BID and the Whole
Study SAF populations.

population with locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC and were consistent across the safety
populations (Table 35). At baseline, the majority of the participants in all safety populations
never had any smoking habits, had an ECOG performance status of 1, had Stage IV disease, and
had no brain metastases. All participants in Group D had a HER2 activating mutation and had
received at least 1 prior line of systemic anti-cancer therapy, as per protocol.

Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments
Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
Data:
Please see The Applicant’s Position below.
The Applicant’s Position:
Dedicated quality assurance and quality control systems were implemented to assure the quality

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. The data submitted was organized and adequate
to perform a complete review of the safety of sevabertinib.
Categorization of Adverse Event
Data and the Applicant’s Position:
Safety evaluations: Generally, the safety evaluations were performed at Screening, during the
Treatment period, at the EoT visit within 7 days after the last administration of sevabertinib, and
at the Safety Follow-Up visit, 30 to 35 days after the last treatment with sevabertinib. During the
Active follow-up (imaging FU visits) period, only (S)AEs related to the study procedures were to
be collected.
TEAE definition: AEs were considered to be treatment-emergent if they had started or worsened
after the first administration of study treatment, up to 30 days after the last administration of
study treatment. (S)AEs were to be collected from the start of study treatment until 30 days after
the last dose of study drug administration. Only TEAEs are reported in this Assessment Aid, and
all tables (descriptive summaries) and discussions relate to the number of patients with TEAEs,
unless explicitly stated otherwise.
MedDRA coding: AEs were coded and are presented using the MedDRA, version 27.1.
Severity grading: The severity of TEAEs was graded using CTCAE v. 5.0, as reported by the
investigators, and is presented by the worst grade observed. Hematological and general
chemistry laboratory abnormalities were graded according to CTCAE v. 5.0 but using laboratory
measurements only, and no signs and symptoms or therapies were considered. Laboratory
parameter hyperglycemia has no numeric values in CTCAE v. 5.0 and was therefore graded
according to CTCAE v. 4.03.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s categorization of AEs. The safety assessment focused on
the frequency and severity of all treatment-emergent AEs (TEAEs). A TEAE was defined as any
AE occurring between the start of treatment and within 30 days after completion of treatment).
AEs that start before first drug intake and worsen under treatment are also considered as
‘treatment-emergent’. Additional analyses included assessment of TEAEs leading to death,
SAEs, and TEAEs leading to dose reduction, interruption, or permanent discontinuation of
sevabertinib. FDA’s independent analyses of these outcomes are presented in Section 8.2.4. The
FDA review was completed using MedDRA preferred terms (PTs) and CTCAE version 5.0
grading criteria.

Routine Clinical Tests
Data:
Details on the safety assessments, including routine clinical laboratory tests (hematology, general
chemistry, and urinalysis), vital signs (temperature, heart rate, respiratory rate, body weight,
BMI, and blood pressure), 12-lead ECGs, cardiac function testing, and physical examinations are
provided in the Study 21607 protocol.
In Study 21607, hematological and general chemistry laboratory abnormalities were graded
according to CTCAE v. 5.0 but using laboratory measurements only, and no signs and symptoms
or therapies were considered. Laboratory parameter hyperglycemia has no numeric values in
CTCAE v. 5.0 and was therefore graded according to CTCAE v. 4.03 (which does incorporate
numeric values). Shift tables “Laboratory abnormalities worsening from baseline” were created.
In addition, for hypokalemia, hyponatremia, lipase increased, and serum amylase increased,
worsening from baseline was summarized using categories obtained from the grade definitions in
CTCAE v. 5.0 but based on laboratory measurements only. Laboratory abnormalities reported as
TEAEs that include clinical assessments are presented in TEAE tables.
The Applicant’s Position:
The methods and time points for collection and analysis of safety assessments including routine
clinical laboratory tests, were appropriate to monitor and assess the safety of sevabertinib in
Study 21607.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.

The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, 90 participants (25.2%) of the 357 in the Whole Study SAF had died, as of the database
cut-off; there were 19 deaths (23.5%) in Group D and 58 deaths (21.6%) in the Whole Study 20
mg BID population (Table 54). The majority of the deaths in the study (65 participants; 18.2%)
occurred >30 days after the last sevabertinib dose. Death (including Grade 5 disease progression)
from the start of treatment up to 30 days after the last dose was reported in 6 participants (7.4%)
in Group D and in 15 participants (5.6%) in the Whole Study 20 mg BID population, with
progressive disease being the most common primary cause of death.
The incidence of participants with fatal TEAEs was consistent across the 3 safety populations
(Table 55). In the Whole Study SAF, 26 participants (7.3%) had a Grade 5 TEAE, with disease
progression being the most common event (13 participants, 3.6%), followed by respiratory
events, of which dyspnea and respiratory failure occurred in >1 participant (0.8% of participants
each).

Sevabertinib (BAY 2927088) – Version 2.0
1 Grade 5 TEAE (cardio-respiratory arrest in participant
) in the Whole Study
20 mg BID population was assessed as related to the study drug by the investigator (Table 56).
This 69-year-old male, with a low body mass index (18.4 kg/m2), and a history of inappetence
since approximately 5 months before starting the study drug, ongoing hypotension and
supraventricular tachycardia (which recovered 7 days after initiation of sevabertinib treatment),
was reported with the following TEAEs starting from Day 38: Grade 2 vomiting, ileus, and
hypokalemia, as well as Grade 1 pneumonia, hypertriglyceridemia, hyperglycemia, blood
bilirubin increased, supraventricular tachycardia, and diarrhea. On Day 42, Grade 3 renal failure
developed, leading to sevabertinib discontinuation; the participant was found to have diffuse
bilateral renal lesions, abnormal, on an ultrasound scan. The participant was hospitalized on Day
44, received medical treatments and incomplete hemodialysis; however, his general condition
worsened. The participant passed away at home on Day 45 due to cardio-respiratory arrest. The
participant's risk of cardio-respiratory arrest was heightened by underlying advanced lung cancer,
emphysema, coronary artery calcification, sinus tachycardia, and left anterior fascicular block.
The participant’s condition was further complicated by renal failure, pneumonia exacerbation,
and concomitant medications (e.g. moxifloxacin, benzylpenicillin, insulin). Importantly, the
participant was found to have diffused bilateral renal lesions; therefore, the role of the study
treatment and renal failure remains unclear.
(b) (6)

Narratives of all participants with fatal TEAEs are provided with the NDA, Module 5.3.5.2,
Report B003047, Section 9.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Although FDA agrees with the listing of deaths provided by the Applicant above in Table 56,
FDA’s review could not exclude a potential contribution of sevabertinib to all fatal adverse
reactions described above. An overall summary of the FDA’s analysis of TEAEs is presented in
FDA Table 57. A summary of deaths amongst the safety populations is presented in FDA Table
58. Comprehensive narrative review and FDA assessment for all cases of death in the primary
safety population is presented in FDA Table 59.
